Image name: POGBa_task2_gt3_PAIR4
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 17
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are H and O.
2.  **Goal:** Find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect H and O. The target nodes (H and O) cannot be removed.
3.  **Analyze direct connections to target nodes:**
    *   Neighbors of H: G, B, I.
    *   Neighbors of O: C, P, Y.
    *   There is no direct edge between H and O.
4.  **Check for a single-node cut:**
    *   To disconnect H from O, any single node removal must break all paths.
    *   Consider removing a neighbor of H (G, B, or I):
        *   If G is removed, paths like H-B-C-O and H-I-P-O still exist.
        *   If B is removed, paths like H-G-F-E-C-O and H-I-P-O still exist.
        *   If I is removed, paths like H-G-F-E-B-C-O and H-B-C-O still exist.
    *   Consider removing a neighbor of O (C, P, or Y):
        *   If C is removed, paths like H-I-P-O and H-B-N-P-O still exist.
        *   If P is removed, paths like H-B-C-O and H-I-C-O still exist.
        *   If Y is removed, paths through C and P still exist (Y is only connected to O and does not lead to H).
    *   Consider removing any other single node (e.g., E, F, J, M, N, X, K, L, D). Due to the graph's connectivity, multiple alternative paths would remain.
    *   Therefore, a single node removal is not sufficient to disconnect H and O. The minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2.
5.  **Search for a two-node cut:**
    *   Let's focus on the nodes directly connected to O: C, P, Y.
    *   Node Y is a leaf node connected only to O. Removing Y would not disconnect O from the rest of the graph, as paths through C and P would still exist.
    *   Consider removing nodes C and P. If both C and P are removed, O's only remaining connection is to Y. Since Y is not connected to any other node that could lead to H, removing C and P would completely isolate O from the rest of the graph (except Y). This would effectively disconnect H from O.
    *   Let's verify this cut set [C, P]:
        *   Any path from H to O *must* pass through either C or P (or Y, but Y is a dead end for paths from H).
        *   If C is removed, all paths that use C to reach O are broken.
        *   If P is removed, all paths that use P to reach O are broken.
        *   Since all paths from H to O must use either C or P, removing both C and P will break all possible paths.
    *   Thus, [C, P] is a valid cut set of size 2.
6.  **Conclusion:** Since we determined that at least 2 nodes must be removed, and we found a valid cut set of size 2 ([C, P]), this is the minimum number of nodes required.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [C, P]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 3, 'cut_set': ['F', 'B', 'I']}

Image name: POGBb_task2_gt2_PAIR5
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 17
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are M and B.
2.  **Identify direct neighbors of M:** M is directly connected to E, L, and J.
3.  **Analyze the role of L:** Node L is only connected to M and E. This means any path from M that goes through L must then pass through E to continue towards B. Therefore, if node E is removed, all paths M-L-... are also effectively broken, as they cannot reach B without passing through E.
4.  **Identify the main "branches" from M:** Based on the connections and the role of L, all paths from M to B must pass through one of two "bottlenecks":
    *   **Branch 1:** Paths starting M -> E (which also covers paths M -> L -> E).
    *   **Branch 2:** Paths starting M -> J.
5.  **Consider a single node removal:**
    *   If we remove E: All paths from Branch 1 are broken. However, paths from Branch 2 (M -> J -> ...) would still exist. So, E alone is not enough.
    *   If we remove J: All paths from Branch 2 are broken. However, paths from Branch 1 (M -> E -> ...) would still exist. So, J alone is not enough.
    *   As established in the examples, a single node must be common to ALL paths to be a cut set of size 1. Since M has multiple independent routes (via E/L and via J), no single node can disconnect them.
6.  **Consider a two-node removal:**
    *   Based on the analysis in step 4, if we remove node E, all paths from M that go through E or L are severed.
    *   If we then also remove node J, all paths from M that go through J are severed.
    *   Since all paths from M to B must pass through either E (or L then E) or J, removing both E and J will ensure that no path remains between M and B.
7.  **Verify the cut set [E, J]:**
    *   **Removing E:** This breaks all paths that start M-E-... (e.g., M-E-B, M-E-F-K-G-H-B, M-E-F-X-G-H-B). It also breaks all paths that start M-L-E-... (e.g., M-L-E-B, M-L-E-F-K-G-H-B, M-L-E-F-X-G-H-B) because L's only connection to the rest of the graph (other than M) is E.
    *   **Removing J:** This breaks all paths that start M-J-... (e.g., M-J-D-P-O-N-B, M-J-P-C-B, M-J-O-Y-N-B, and all other complex paths originating from J).
    *   With both E and J removed, M has no remaining connections to the rest of the graph that could lead to B.
8.  **Conclusion:** The minimum number of nodes to remove is 2, and a valid cut set is [E, J].

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [E, J]

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are H and O. I need to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect H and O. I cannot remove H or O.

2.  **Explore paths from H to O:** I will systematically list paths from H to O to understand the graph's connectivity. I'll look for distinct "branches" or "bottlenecks".

    *   **Paths starting H -> B:**
        *   H - B - C - O
        *   H - B - N - C - O
        *   H - B - N - Y - O

    *   **Paths starting H -> I:**
        *   H - I - C - O
        *   H - I - J - P - O
        *   H - I - J - D - P - O

    *   **Paths starting H -> G:**
        *   H - G - K - J - P - O
        *   H - G - K - J - D - P - O
        *   H - G - F - K - J - P - O
        *   H - G - F - K - J - D - P - O
        *   H - G - F - X - K - J - P - O
        *   H - G - F - X - K - J - D - P - O
        *   H - G - F - M - L - E - B - N - Y - O (This path loops through B and N)
        *   H - G - F - M - L - E - B - N - C - O (This path loops through B and N)
        *   H - G - F - M - L - E - B - C - O (This path loops through B)

3.  **Attempt to find a cut set of size 1:**
    *   If I remove B, paths through G and I remain.
    *   If I remove G, paths through B and I remain.
    *   If I remove I, paths through B and G remain.
    *   If I remove C, paths H-B-N-Y-O, H-I-J-P-O, H-G-K-J-P-O, etc. remain.
    *   If I remove J, paths H-B-C-O, H-B-N-C-O, H-B-N-Y-O, H-I-C-O, etc. remain.
    *   If I remove N, paths H-B-C-O, H-I-C-O, H-I-J-P-O, H-G-K-J-P-O, etc. remain.
    *   No single node removal disconnects H and O. Therefore, the minimum cut set size is at least 2.

4.  **Attempt to find a cut set of size 2:**
    *   Consider removing two nodes. For example, [C, J]:
        *   Paths through C are cut (H-B-C-O, H-I-C-O).
        *   Paths through J are cut (all paths to P, e.g., H-I-J-P-O, H-G-K-J-P-O).
        *   However, paths like H-B-N-Y-O and H-G-F-M-L-E-B-N-Y-O remain connected. So [C, J] is not enough.
    *   Consider removing [C, N]:
        *   Paths through C are cut.
        *   Paths through N are cut (H-B-N-Y-O, H-B-N-C-O).
        *   However, paths through J and P (e.g., H-I-J-P-O, H-G-K-J-P-O) remain connected. So [C, N] is not enough.
    *   Consider removing [J, N]:
        *   Paths through J are cut (all paths to P).
        *   Paths through N are cut (H-B-N-Y-O, H-B-N-C-O).
        *   However, paths through C not involving N (H-B-C-O, H-I-C-O) remain connected. So [J, N] is not enough.
    *   It appears a cut set of size 2 is not sufficient. This suggests the minimum cut set size is at least 3.

5.  **Identify vertex-disjoint paths (Menger's Theorem):** To confirm the minimum cut size, I will look for vertex-disjoint paths between H and O (excluding H and O themselves).
    *   **Path 1:** H - B - C - O (Intermediate nodes: {B, C})
    *   **Path 2:** H - I - J - P - O (Intermediate nodes: {I, J, P})
    *   **Path 3:** H - G - F - M - L - E - N - Y - O (Intermediate nodes: {G, F, M, L, E, N, Y})

    These three paths are vertex-disjoint in their intermediate nodes. According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes to remove to disconnect H and O is equal to the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between them. Since I found 3 vertex-disjoint paths, the minimum cut set size is 3.

6.  **Formulate a cut set of size 3:** Based on the three disjoint paths, I can select one node from each path's intermediate nodes to form a cut set.
    *   To cut Path 1 (H-B-C-O), I can remove C.
    *   To cut Path 2 (H-I-J-P-O), I can remove J.
    *   To cut Path 3 (H-G-F-M-L-E-N-Y-O), I can remove N.

    Let's verify the cut set [C, J, N]:
    *   Removing C disconnects all paths that directly use C to reach O (H-B-C-O, H-I-C-O).
    *   Removing J disconnects all paths that use J to reach P, and thus O (H-I-J-P-O, H-G-K-J-P-O, etc.). Since P is only connected to J and D, and D is only connected to J and P, removing J effectively isolates P from H.
    *   Removing N disconnects all paths that use N to reach Y or C (H-B-N-Y-O, H-B-N-C-O, H-G-F-M-L-E-B-N-Y-O, H-G-F-M-L-E-B-N-C-O). Since Y is only connected to N and O, removing N effectively isolates Y from H.

    With C, J, and N removed, O is no longer connected to H through any path.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [C, J, N]

Image name: Tu_task2_gt2_PAIR3
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 17
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  **Identify Target Nodes:** The two black nodes are N and G. I cannot remove these nodes.

2.  **Initial Scan for Single Node Cut:** I first check if removing any single node (other than N or G) can disconnect N from G.
    *   If I remove P: Paths like N-C-H-G would remain.
    *   If I remove H: Paths like N-O-P-F-G would remain.
    *   If I remove F: Paths like N-C-H-G would remain.
    *   If I remove I: Paths like N-C-H-G and N-O-P-F-G would remain.
    *   If I remove C: Paths like N-B-I-H-G and N-O-P-F-G would remain.
    *   If I remove B: Paths like N-C-H-G and N-O-P-F-G would remain.
    *   If I remove O: Paths like N-C-H-G and N-B-I-H-G would remain.
    *   If I remove E: Paths like N-O-P-F-G and N-C-H-G would remain.
    No single node removal is sufficient to disconnect N from G.

3.  **Identify Vertex-Disjoint Paths:** To determine the minimum number of nodes required, I look for vertex-disjoint paths between N and G.
    *   **Path 1:** N - O - P - F - G (Intermediate nodes: O, P, F)
    *   **Path 2:** N - C - H - G (Intermediate nodes: C, H)
    These two paths are internally vertex-disjoint (i.e., the intermediate nodes {O, P, F} are distinct from {C, H}). According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes to remove to disconnect N from G must be at least 2.

4.  **Search for a Cut Set of Size 3:** Since a cut set of size 1 is not possible, and the minimum is at least 2, I will look for a cut set of size 3. The goal is to find a set of nodes whose removal breaks all paths from N to G. All paths from N to G must eventually pass through either H or F (the direct neighbors of G, excluding K and X which are only connected to G and F). Therefore, I need to find nodes that effectively cut off access to both H and F from N.

    Let's consider the set of nodes {P, E, H}.
    *   **Removing H:** This immediately breaks all paths from N to G that pass through H.
        *   Examples: N-C-H-G, N-C-B-I-H-G, N-B-I-H-G, N-O-C-H-G, N-O-C-B-I-H-G.
        After removing H, any remaining path from N to G must go through F.
    *   **Removing P and E:** Now, I need to ensure no path can reach F from N. F is directly connected to P and E (besides G, K, X). If both P and E are removed, then F becomes isolated from the rest of the graph (except for G, K, X).
        *   Paths that would go through P (e.g., N-O-P-F-G, N-C-B-I-P-F-G, N-B-I-P-F-G) are broken by removing P.
        *   Paths that would go through E (e.g., N-C-B-E-F-G, N-B-E-F-G) are broken by removing E.
        *   Paths that would go through both E and P (e.g., N-C-B-E-P-F-G, N-B-E-P-F-G) are broken by removing E or P.

    Since removing H breaks all paths to G via H, and removing P and E breaks all paths to G via F, the set {P, E, H} successfully disconnects N from G.

5.  **Conclusion:** I have found a cut set of size 3 ({P, E, H}). Since I previously established that the minimum cut size is at least 2, a cut set of size 3 is the minimum possible.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [P, E, H]
--- Ground Truth ---

    *   Queue: `[(N, [N])]`
    *   Visited: `{}`

2.  **Level 1:** Explore neighbors of N.
    *   Dequeue `(N, [N])`.
    *   Neighbors of N are Y, O, C, B.
    *   Add these to the queue with their paths:
        *   `(Y, [N, Y])`
        *   `(O, [N, O])`
        *   `(C, [N, C])`
        *   `(B, [N, B])`
    *   Visited: `{N}`

3.  **Level 2:** Explore neighbors of Level 1 nodes.
    *   Dequeue `(Y, [N, Y])`. Neighbors: N, O. N is visited. O is in queue.
    *   Dequeue `(O, [N, O])`. Neighbors: N, Y, C, P. N, Y are visited. C is in queue. P is new.
        *   Add `(P, [N, O, P])` to queue.
    *   Dequeue `(C, [N, C])`. Neighbors: N, O, B, H, I. N, O, B are visited or in queue. H, I are new.
        *   Add `(H, [N, C, H])` to queue.
        *   Add `(I, [N, C, I])` to queue.
    *   Dequeue `(B, [N, B])`. Neighbors: N, C, E, H. N, C are visited. H is in queue. E is new.
        *   Add `(E, [N, B, E])` to queue.
    *   Visited: `{N, Y, O, C, B}`

4.  **Level 3:** Explore neighbors of Level 2 nodes.
    *   Dequeue `(P, [N, O, P])`. Neighbors: O, E, F, I, J, D. O is visited. E, I are in queue. F, J, D are new.
        *   Add `(F, [N, O, P, F])` to queue.
        *   Add `(J, [N, O, P, J])` to queue.
        *   Add `(D, [N, O, P, D])` to queue.
    *   Dequeue `(H, [N, C, H])`. Neighbors: C, B, I, G. C, B are visited. I is in queue. **G is the target node!**
        *   Path found: `[N, C, H, G]`
        *   Length: 3

Since BFS explores layer by layer, the first time the target node G is reached, the path found is guaranteed to be the shortest.

2.  **Consider the impact of removing these edges:**
    *   If I remove the edge (L, E), any path from L to J that would have started with L-E-... is broken. However, paths starting with L-M-... would still exist (e.g., L-M-J).
    *   If I remove the edge (L, M), any path from L to J that would have started with L-M-... is broken. However, paths starting with L-E-... would still exist (e.g., L-E-I-J).

3.  **Determine if a single edge removal is sufficient:** Based on step 2, removing only one edge (either (L, E) or (L, M)) is not sufficient to disconnect L from J, as there would always be an alternative path through the other remaining edge from L. Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove must be at least 2.

4.  **Explore a cut set of size 2:**

Since L cannot connect to any other node, it cannot connect to J. This successfully disconnects L and J. This cut set is [(L, E), (L, M)] and has a size of 2.

        - Path L-M-J is broken by (M, J).
        - Path L-E-M-J is broken by (M, J).
        - Path L-E-I-J is broken by (I, J).
        - However, paths like L-E-I-P-J still exist because (P, J) is not removed. So, this is not a valid cut set.

        Let's consider the cut set [(L, E), (M, J)]:

        - The direct path L-M-J is broken by removing (M, J).
        - Any path L-M-E-... is also broken because (L, E) is removed.
        This cut set also successfully disconnects L and J and has a size of 2.

5.  **Conclusion:** Since 1 edge is not sufficient, and we have found multiple cut sets of size 2 that successfully disconnect L and J, the minimum number of edges to remove is 2. I will provide one of the valid cut sets.

The target nodes are N and G. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect N from G.

    *   Neighbors of N: Y, O, B
    *   Neighbors of G: K, X, H

2.  **Analyze the paths from N to G:**

3.  **Consider the "entry points" to G:**
    *   **Paths to K:** K is connected to F and G. Therefore, any path from N to K must pass through F.
    *   **Paths to X:** X is connected to F and G. Therefore, any path from N to X must pass through F.
    *   **Paths to H:** H is connected to B, I, and G. Therefore, any path from N to H must pass through B or I.

4.  **Look for potential bottleneck edges:**
    *   **For paths to K and X:** Both K and X are connected to F. F itself is connected to E, K, and X. This means that any path from N to K or X must first reach F. The only edge connecting F to the rest of the graph (from N's side) is (E, F). If we remove the edge (E, F), then F becomes disconnected from N, and consequently, K and X also become disconnected from N (as their only other connection is to G).
    *   **For paths to H:** Any path from N to H must eventually reach H. To disconnect H from G, we can remove the edge (H, G).

5.  **Propose a cut set based on these observations:**
    Let's consider removing the edges (E, F) and (H, G).
    *   **Removing (E, F):** This edge is crucial because it's the sole connection point for node F to the rest of the graph (from N's side). Since K and X are only connected to F (and G), removing (E, F) effectively disconnects all paths from N to K and all paths from N to X.
        *   Example path cut: N - B - E - F - K - G (cut at (E, F))
        *   Example path cut: N - B - E - F - X - G (cut at (E, F))
        *   Example path cut: N - O - P - J - M - E - F - K - G (cut at (E, F))
        *   Example path cut: N - O - P - J - M - E - F - X - G (cut at (E, F))
    *   **Removing (H, G):** This edge directly connects H to G. By removing it, all paths from N that reach H are prevented from continuing to G.
        *   Example path cut: N - B - H - G (cut at (H, G))
        *   Example path cut: N - B - C - I - H - G (cut at (H, G))
        *   Example path cut: N - O - C - I - H - G (cut at (H, G))

6.  **Verify the proposed cut set:**
    After removing (E, F) and (H, G):
    *   No path can reach K from N because the connection to F (via E) is broken.
    *   No path can reach X from N because the connection to F (via E) is broken.
    *   No path can reach G via H because the direct connection (H, G) is broken.
    Therefore, no path exists between N and G.

7.  **Determine minimality:**
    *   A single edge removal is not sufficient:
        *   Removing (E, F) leaves paths like N - B - H - G open.
        *   Removing (H, G) leaves paths like N - B - E - F - K - G open.
        *   Removing any other single edge would also leave multiple paths open.
    *   Since a cut of size 1 is not possible, and we found a cut of size 2, the minimum number of edges to remove is 2.

    *   To cut paths from E that go via M: Remove edge (E,M).
    *   To cut paths from H that go via B: Remove edge (H,B).
    *   To cut paths from H that go via I: Remove edge (H,I).

    *   If a path from G goes through E:
        *   If it goes G-...-E-M-..., it is cut by removing (E,M).
        *   If it goes G-...-E-H-..., it then reaches H. From H, all paths to N must use either (H,B) or (H,I). Since both (H,B) and (H,I) are removed, no path from H can reach N. Thus, paths through E-H are also cut.
    *   If a path from G goes through H:
        *   If it goes G-...-H-B-N, it is cut by removing (H,B).
        *   If it goes G-...-H-I-..., it is cut by removing (H,I).
    Since all paths from G to N must pass through E or H, and all branches from E and H leading to N are cut by these three edges, the set [(E,M), (H,B), (H,I)] successfully disconnects G from N.

4.  **Prove minimality using Menger's Theorem:**
    To prove that 3 is the minimum number of edges, I need to find at least 3 edge-disjoint paths between G and N.

    *   **Path 1:** G - H - B - N
        *   Edges: {(G,H), (H,B), (B,N)}

    *   Path 3 uses (G,F), (F,E), (E,H), (H,I), (I,C), (C,N).
    Upon inspection, there are no common edges between any pair of these three paths. They are indeed edge-disjoint.

    According to Menger's Theorem, the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between two nodes is equal to the minimum number of edges required to disconnect them. Since we found 3 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum cut size must be at least 3. As we found a cut set of size 3, this is the minimum.

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are H and E. My goal is to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect H and E, without removing H or E themselves.

First, I identify all direct connections to the target nodes.
Node H is connected to G, I, C.
Node E is connected to P, B, L, M.

Nodes L and M are only connected to E and each other. They do not provide any path from H to E, as they are not connected to any other nodes in the graph that could lead to H. Therefore, any path from H to E must pass through either P or B to reach E.

Let's consider the implications of this observation:
1.  If I remove node P, all paths that go through P to reach E will be severed. However, paths that go through B to reach E will remain.
2.  If I remove node B, all paths that go through B to reach E will be severed. However, paths that go through P to reach E will remain.

Since E is connected to the rest of the graph *only* via P and B (excluding L and M), to completely disconnect E from H, both P and B must be removed.

Let's verify this:
*   **Paths from H to E via P:**
    *   H-G-F-P-E
    *   H-G-K-F-P-E
    *   H-G-X-F-P-E
    *   H-I-P-E
    *   H-I-J-P-E
    *   H-C-N-O-P-E
    *   H-C-O-P-E
    Removing P will break all these paths.

*   **Paths from H to E via B:**
    *   H-I-C-B-E
    *   H-C-B-E
    *   H-C-N-B-E
    *   H-C-O-N-B-E
    Removing B will break all these paths.

Since all paths from H to E must pass through either P or B, removing both P and B will ensure that no path remains between H and E.

Now, I need to confirm that this is the minimum number of nodes.
*   If I remove only one node (e.g., P), paths through B would still exist (e.g., H-C-B-E).
*   If I remove only one node (e.g., B), paths through P would still exist (e.g., H-I-P-E).
Therefore, a single node removal is not sufficient.

Thus, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 2. The cut set is [P, B].

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [P, B]

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.

First, I will identify all possible paths from G to I.
Let's list the direct neighbors of G: X, K, F, H.
Let's list the direct neighbors of I: H, E, C, J, P.

Notice that node H is a direct neighbor of both G and I. This means there is a direct path G-H-I.
If H is not removed, this path G-H-I will always exist, connecting G and I. Therefore, to disconnect G and I, node H *must* be removed. This establishes that any minimal cut set must include H.

Now, let's assume H is removed. We need to find the minimum number of additional nodes to remove to disconnect G from I.
After removing H, the connections involving H are severed.
- G is now connected to X, K, F.
- I is now connected to E, C, J, P.
- B is now connected to C, E (its connection to H is gone).

Let's trace all remaining paths from G to I after H is removed:
All paths from G must now go through F, K, or X.
- Paths starting G -> F:
    - G -> F -> E -> I
    - G -> F -> E -> M -> J -> I
    - G -> F -> E -> M -> J -> P -> I
    - G -> F -> K -> X (K and X are connected to F, so paths like G-F-K-F-E-I are possible, but they still pass through F and E).
- Paths starting G -> K:
    - G -> K -> F -> E -> I (and its variations through M, J, P)
- Paths starting G -> X:
    - G -> X -> K -> F -> E -> I (and its variations through M, J, P)
    - G -> X -> F -> E -> I (and its variations through M, J, P)

Observing all these remaining paths, every single one of them passes through the sequence F -> E.
Therefore, to break all these remaining paths, we must remove either node F or node E.

Combining this with our earlier finding that H must be removed, we have two possible minimal cut sets of size 2:
1. Remove H and F: [H, F]
   - Removing H breaks G-H-I, G-H-B-C-I, G-H-B-E-I.
   - Removing F breaks G-F-E-I, G-F-E-M-J-I, G-F-E-M-J-P-I, and any paths involving K or X that lead to F and then E.
   - All paths are broken.

2. Remove H and E: [H, E]
   - Removing H breaks G-H-I, G-H-B-C-I, G-H-B-E-I.
   - Removing E breaks G-F-E-I, G-F-E-M-J-I, G-F-E-M-J-P-I, G-H-B-E-I (already broken by H), and any paths involving K or X that lead to F and then E.
   - All paths are broken.

Both [H, F] and [H, E] are valid cut sets of size 2. Since we've established that H must be removed, and then at least one more node (F or E) must be removed, a cut set of size 1 is not possible. Thus, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 2.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [H, E]

    *   **Path 3:** G - F - E - B - C - I

    *   Intermediate nodes in Path 2: {X, K, J, P}
    *   Intermediate nodes in Path 3: {F, E, B, C}

    These three sets of intermediate nodes are mutually exclusive. This means that Path 1, Path 2, and Path 3 are vertex-disjoint.

4.  **Apply Menger's Theorem:** Menger's Theorem states that the minimum number of vertices that must be removed to disconnect two non-adjacent vertices (G and I) is equal to the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between them. Since I have found 3 vertex-disjoint paths, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

5.  **Determine a minimal cut set:** To disconnect G and I, I must remove at least one intermediate node from each of these three vertex-disjoint paths.

    *   From Path 3 (G - F - E - B - C - I), I can remove node B (or F, E, C). Let's choose B.

    Therefore, a cut set of size 3 is [H, J, B].

6.  **Verify the cut set:** I will confirm that removing nodes H, J, and B indeed breaks all paths between G and I.
    *   Path G - H - I is broken by removing H.
    *   Path G - X - K - J - P - I is broken by removing J.
    *   Path G - F - E - B - C - I is broken by removing B.

        *   G - X - F - E - B - C - I: Broken by removing B.
        *   G - F - X - K - J - P - I: Broken by removing J.

    All identified paths are successfully broken by removing H, J, and B. Since we established that 3 is the minimum number of nodes required, [H, J, B] is a valid minimal cut set.

Step-by-step reasoning.

    *   Node F is connected to K, X, G, E.
    *   Node M is connected to L, E, J.

2.  **Analyze paths from F to M:**
    All paths from F to M must pass through one of M's neighbors that are also connected to the rest of the graph leading to F. These neighbors are E and J. Node L is connected to M, but L is not connected to any other node that could lead back to F, so paths through L are not relevant for disconnecting F and M.

3.  **Consider a cut set by isolating M:**
    If we remove all edges connecting M to nodes that are part of paths from F, M will be disconnected. These edges are (E, M) and (J, M).

4.  **Verify the proposed cut set [(E, M), (J, M)]:**
    *   Removing (E, M) breaks any direct path F-E-M. It also breaks any longer path that eventually reaches E and then M (e.g., F-G-H-E-M).
    *   Removing (J, M) breaks any path that reaches J and then M (e.g., F-G-H-I-J-M, F-G-H-I-P-J-M, etc.).
    *   Since E and J are the only nodes through which paths from F can reach M, removing both (E, M) and (J, M) ensures that no path exists between F and M.

5.  **Determine minimality:**
    *   If only one edge is removed, for example (E, M), then paths through J (e.g., F-G-H-I-J-M) would still connect F and M.
    *   If only one edge is removed, for example (J, M), then paths through E (e.g., F-E-M) would still connect F and M.
    *   Therefore, at least two edges must be removed.

6.  **Conclusion:** The minimum number of edges to remove is 2. The cut set [(E, M), (J, M)] achieves this disconnection. An alternative minimal cut set could be [(F, E), (F, G)], as removing these two edges would isolate F from E and G, thus breaking all paths to M.

Image name: Tu_task2_gt2_PAIR1
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 17
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are B and M.
2.  **Analyze connections from M:** Node M is connected to L, E, and J.
    *   If a path goes M -> L, it must then go L -> E. So, M -> L -> E.
    *   Therefore, any path from M to B must pass through either node E or node J. This means that to disconnect B and M, we must remove at least one node from the set {E, J} AND ensure that the other path is also broken.

3.  **Consider removing E:**
    *   If node E is removed, all paths from M that go through E (M-E-B and M-L-E-B) are severed.
    *   Now, M can only reach B via node J. So, we need to find all paths from B to J and identify common nodes that can disconnect them.
    *   Let's list paths from B to J:
        *   B -> C -> O -> P -> J
        *   B -> C -> H -> I -> P -> J
        *   B -> C -> H -> G -> F -> P -> J
        *   B -> N -> O -> P -> J
        *   B -> O -> P -> J
        *   B -> H -> I -> P -> J
        *   B -> H -> G -> F -> P -> J
    *   Observing these paths, every single path from B to J passes through node P. Therefore, removing P will disconnect B from J.
    *   So, if E is removed, removing P will disconnect B from M. This gives a cut set of [E, P], with a size of 2.

4.  **Consider removing J:**
    *   If node J is removed, all paths from M that go through J (M-J-...) are severed.
    *   Now, M can only reach B via node E (or L, which then goes to E). So, we need to find all paths from B to E and identify common nodes that can disconnect them.
    *   Let's list paths from B to E:
        *   B -> E (direct connection)
        *   B -> C -> O -> P -> E
        *   B -> C -> H -> I -> P -> E
        *   B -> C -> H -> G -> F -> P -> E
        *   B -> N -> O -> P -> E
        *   B -> O -> P -> E
        *   B -> H -> I -> P -> E
        *   B -> H -> G -> F -> P -> E
    *   To disconnect B from E, we would need to remove E itself (which is a direct connection) AND P (to break all indirect paths through P).
    *   So, if J is removed, we would need to remove E and P to disconnect B from M. This gives a cut set of [J, E, P], with a size of 3.

5.  **Compare cut sets:** The cut set [E, P] has a size of 2, which is smaller than [J, E, P] (size 3).
6.  **Verify minimality:**
    *   Can we disconnect B and M with only 1 node?
        *   Removing E leaves paths through J (e.g., B-O-P-J-M).
        *   Removing J leaves paths through E (e.g., B-E-M).
        *   Removing P leaves paths through E (e.g., B-E-M).
        *   No single node removal disconnects B and M.
    *   Therefore, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 2.

The minimal cut set is [E, P].

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [E, P]

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are E and I. My goal is to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect E and I, without removing E or I themselves.

2.  **Analyze direct connections from E:**
    Node E has four direct neighbors: L, M, F, and B. Any path from E to I must start by traversing one of these four edges:
    *   E -> L
    *   E -> M
    *   E -> F
    *   E -> B

3.  **Trace paths from E through its neighbors:**
    *   **Paths starting E -> L:** From L, the only connection (not back to E) is to M. So, any path E -> L -> ... must continue E -> L -> M. From M, the only connection (not back to L or E) is to F. Therefore, any path starting E -> L must pass through F (E -> L -> M -> F).
    *   **Paths starting E -> M:** From M, the only connection (not back to E or L) is to F. Therefore, any path starting E -> M must pass through F (E -> M -> F).
    *   **Paths starting E -> F:** This path already passes through F.
    *   **Paths starting E -> B:** This path already passes through B.

4.  **Identify critical nodes:**
    From the analysis above, it is clear that:
    *   All paths originating from E via L, M, or F must eventually pass through node F.
    *   All paths originating from E via B must pass through node B.

5.  **Formulate a cut set:**
    Since every single path from E to I must pass through either node F or node B, removing both F and B will guarantee that no path remains between E and I.

6.  **Check for minimality:**
    *   If only F is removed, paths like E -> B -> H -> I or E -> B -> C -> I would still exist.
    *   If only B is removed, paths like E -> F -> G -> H -> I or E -> F -> K -> J -> I would still exist.
    Therefore, removing a single node is not sufficient to disconnect E and I.

7.  **Conclusion:** The minimum number of nodes to remove is 2, and the set [F, B] achieves this disconnection.

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are F (black) and J (black). My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between F and J.

1.  **Identify all direct neighbors of F:** F is connected to X, K, G, and E.
2.  **Identify all direct neighbors of J:** J is connected to M, I, P, and D.

3.  **Analyze paths from F to J:** All paths from F to J must start with one of the edges connected to F: (F, X), (F, K), (F, G), or (F, E).

    *   **Paths starting with (F, E):** Any path that begins F-E-... will be broken if the edge (F, E) is removed. Examples include F-E-M-J, F-E-L-M-J, F-E-I-J, F-E-I-P-J.

    *   **Paths starting with (F, G), (F, K), or (F, X):**
        *   If a path starts with (F, G), it reaches node G.
        *   If a path starts with (F, K), it reaches node K. From K, the only way to proceed towards J is via G (K-G).
        *   If a path starts with (F, X), it reaches node X. From X, the only way to proceed towards J is via G (X-G).
        *   Therefore, any path from F that does *not* use the edge (F, E) must eventually pass through node G.

4.  **Identify the bottleneck from G to the rest of the graph (towards J):**
    *   Node G is connected to F, X, K, and H.
    *   From G, the only edge that leads to the "middle" or "J-side" of the graph (i.e., to nodes other than F, X, K) is the edge (G, H).
    *   Therefore, any path from F that goes through G (or K or X, which lead to G) must traverse the edge (G, H) to continue towards J.

5.  **Propose a cut set:**
    Based on the analysis above, all paths from F to J fall into one of two categories:
    *   Paths that start with (F, E).
    *   Paths that go through G and then (G, H).

    If we remove the edge (F, E), all paths of the first category are broken.
    If we remove the edge (G, H), all paths of the second category are broken.

    Therefore, the cut set **[(F, E), (G, H)]** should disconnect F from J.

6.  **Verify the cut set:**
    *   **Path F-E-M-J:** Broken by removing (F, E).
    *   **Path F-E-L-M-J:** Broken by removing (F, E).
    *   **Path F-E-I-J:** Broken by removing (F, E).
    *   **Path F-E-I-P-J:** Broken by removing (F, E).
    *   **Path F-G-H-I-J:** Broken by removing (G, H).
    *   **Path F-G-H-B-C-O-P-J:** Broken by removing (G, H).
    *   **Path F-K-G-H-I-J:** Broken by removing (G, H).
    *   **Path F-X-G-H-B-C-N-O-P-J:** Broken by removing (G, H).
    All identified paths are broken.

7.  **Verify minimality:**
    *   If only (F, E) is removed, paths like F-G-H-I-J still exist.
    *   If only (G, H) is removed, paths like F-E-M-J still exist.
    Since removing either edge alone is not sufficient, both edges are necessary. Thus, a cut of size 1 is not possible, and 2 is the minimum.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(F, E), (G, H)]

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are N and H. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect N from H.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their immediate neighbors:**
    *   Node N (black) is connected to: Y, O, C, B.
    *   Node H (black) is connected to: G, I, C, B.

2.  **Identify edge-disjoint paths between N and H:**
    To determine the minimum number of edges to remove, I will use Menger's Theorem, which states that the minimum number of edges in an edge cut is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between the two nodes. I will look for paths that do not share any common edges.

    *   **Path 1:** N - B - H
        Edges: (N, B), (B, H)

    *   **Path 2:** N - C - H
        Edges: (N, C), (C, H)

    *   **Path 3:** N - O - P - F - G - H
        Edges: (N, O), (O, P), (P, F), (F, G), (G, H)

3.  **Verify edge-disjointness of the identified paths:**
    *   (N, B) is only in Path 1.
    *   (B, H) is only in Path 1.
    *   (N, C) is only in Path 2.
    *   (C, H) is only in Path 2.
    *   (N, O) is only in Path 3.
    *   (O, P) is only in Path 3.
    *   (P, F) is only in Path 3.
    *   (F, G) is only in Path 3.
    *   (G, H) is only in Path 3.

    All three paths are indeed edge-disjoint. This means that any set of edges that disconnects N from H must contain at least one edge from each of these three paths. Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is at least 3.

4.  **Find a cut set of size 3:**
    Since the minimum number of edges is at least 3, I now need to find a set of 3 edges whose removal completely disconnects N from H.

    Consider the edges directly connected to N that lead to the rest of the graph (excluding Y, which is a dead-end for paths to H):
    *   (N, B)
    *   (N, C)
    *   (N, O)

    If these three edges are removed:
    *   Node N would only be connected to Node Y.
    *   Node Y is only connected to Node N.
    *   Therefore, N would be completely isolated from the rest of the graph, including H.

    This set of edges [(N, B), (N, C), (N, O)] successfully disconnects N from H. Its size is 3.

5.  **Conclusion:**
    Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum cut size is at least 3. I have also identified a cut set of 3 edges that successfully disconnects N from H. Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(N, B), (N, C), (N, O)]

1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The black nodes are B and M.

2.  **Identify direct connections to M:** Node M is directly connected to E, J, and L. Therefore, any path from B to M must pass through one of these three nodes (E, J, or L).

3.  **Consider a cut by isolating M:** A straightforward approach is to remove all edges directly connected to M. These are (E, M), (J, M), and (L, M). This would be a cut of size 3. We need to check if a smaller cut is possible.

4.  **Explore paths from B to M:**
    Let's list the immediate neighbors of B: C, E, H, I, N, O.
    Let's list the immediate neighbors of M: E, J, L.

    All paths from B to M must eventually reach E, J, or L, and then M.

    *   **Paths through E to M:**
        *   B - E - M
        *   B - E - L - M
        *   Paths from B to P, then P - E - M (e.g., B - I - P - E - M)
        *   Paths from B to P, then P - E - L - M (e.g., B - I - P - E - L - M)

    *   **Paths through J to M:**
        *   Paths from B to P, then P - J - M (e.g., B - I - P - J - M)

    *   **Paths through L to M:**
        *   Paths from B to E, then E - L - M (e.g., B - E - L - M)
        *   Paths from B to P, then P - E - L - M (e.g., B - I - P - E - L - M)

5.  **Attempt to find a cut of size 1:**
    *   Removing (E, M): Paths through J (e.g., B-I-P-J-M) and L (e.g., B-E-L-M, if E-L-M is considered a path to M not directly through E-M) would still exist. So, (E, M) alone is not enough.
    *   Removing (J, M): Paths through E (e.g., B-E-M) and L (e.g., B-E-L-M) would still exist. So, (J, M) alone is not enough.
    *   Removing (L, M): Paths through E (e.g., B-E-M) and J (e.g., B-I-P-J-M) would still exist. So, (L, M) alone is not enough.
    Therefore, a cut of size 1 is not possible.

6.  **Attempt to find a cut of size 2:**
    Let's consider removing two edges.

    *   **Option A: Remove (E, M) and (J, M).**
        *   **Impact of removing (E, M):**
            *   All direct paths from E to M (like B-E-M, B-I-P-E-M) are broken.
            *   Crucially, node L is connected to M via (L, M) and to E via (L, E). Any path from B to L must pass through E (e.g., B-E-L or B-I-P-E-L). If (E, M) is removed, then E cannot reach M. Since L's only connection to the "B-side" of the graph is through E, and E can no longer reach M, any path B-...-E-L-M is effectively broken because E cannot pass the connection to M.
        *   **Impact of removing (J, M):**
            *   All direct paths from J to M (like B-I-P-J-M) are broken.
        *   **Conclusion for Option A:** With (E, M) and (J, M) removed, M cannot be reached from E, and M cannot be reached from J. Furthermore, L cannot serve as a bridge from B to M because its only connection to the B-side is through E, and E's path to M is cut. Therefore, M is completely disconnected from B. This is a cut of size 2.

    *   **Option B: Remove (E, M) and (L, M).**
        *   This cuts paths through E and L to M. However, paths through J to M (e.g., B-I-P-J-M) would still exist. So, this is not a valid cut set.

    *   **Option C: Remove (J, M) and (L, M).**
        *   This cuts paths through J and L to M. However, paths through E to M (e.g., B-E-M) would still exist. So, this is not a valid cut set.

    *   **Option D: Consider edges further up the graph, e.g., (B, E) and (P, J).**
        *   Removing (B, E) cuts the direct path B-E-M.
        *   Removing (P, J) cuts paths from P to J.
        *   However, a path like B-I-P-E-M would still exist. So, this is not a valid cut set.

7.  **Final Verification:** The cut set [(E, M), (J, M)] successfully disconnects B and M. No smaller set of edges can achieve this.

The minimum number of edges to remove is 2.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(E, M), (J, M)]

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are J and F. My goal is to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect J and F. I cannot remove J or F.

First, I will identify all possible paths from J to F.
The direct neighbors of J are D, P, M, I.
The direct neighbors of F are E, K, X, G, H.

Let's categorize paths based on the first node from J:

**Paths starting J -> D:**
All paths starting J-D must pass through P, then O. From O, they can go to N or Y. Both N and Y connect to N, which then connects to B. From B, paths can go to E or C.
- If B -> E: J - D - P - O - N - B - E - F (and J - D - P - O - Y - N - B - E - F)
- If B -> C: J - D - P - O - N - B - C - H - F (and J - D - P - O - Y - N - B - C - H - F). From H, there are further connections to F (H-F, H-X-F, H-G-X-F, H-G-K-F). So, all paths through C eventually go through H.

**Paths starting J -> M:**
J - M - L - E - F.
J - M - L - E - B - C - H - F (and its variations through X, G, K from H).

**Paths starting J -> I:**
J - I - H - F (and its variations through X, G, K from H).
J - I - H - C - B - E - F.

Now, let's analyze the "bottleneck" nodes.
Notice that all paths to F must eventually pass through one of F's direct neighbors: E, K, X, G, H.
Let's examine the connections to K, X, G:
- K is connected to F, G, X.
- X is connected to F, K, G, H.
- G is connected to K, X, H.
Crucially, to reach K, X, or G from the rest of the graph (excluding F itself), one must pass through H. For example, to reach K, you can come from G or X. Both G and X are connected to H. There are no other connections to K, X, G that bypass H.
Therefore, any path from J to F that does not go through E, must go through H (to reach F directly, or via K, X, G).

This implies that every path from J to F must pass through either node E or node H.

Let's verify this:
1.  **Paths through E:**
    *   J - M - L - E - F
    *   J - D - P - O - N - B - E - F
    *   J - D - P - O - Y - N - B - E - F
    *   J - I - H - C - B - E - F
2.  **Paths through H:**
    *   J - I - H - F
    *   J - I - H - X - F
    *   J - I - H - G - X - F
    *   J - I - H - G - K - F
    *   J - D - P - O - N - B - C - H - F (and its variations through X, G, K from H)
    *   J - D - P - O - Y - N - B - C - H - F (and its variations through X, G, K from H)

Since every path from J to F must pass through either E or H, removing both E and H will disconnect J from F. This gives a cut set of size 2: {E, H}.

Now, I need to confirm that a single node removal is not sufficient.
- If I remove E: Paths like J-I-H-F still exist.
- If I remove H: Paths like J-M-L-E-F still exist.
- If I remove any other single node (D, P, M, I, O, N, B, C, X, K, G, L, Y), there will always be at least one path through E and at least one path through H that remain. For example, removing B still leaves J-M-L-E-F and J-I-H-F.

Since a single node removal is not enough, and removing {E, H} disconnects J and F, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 2.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [E, H]

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect the two black nodes, M and B.

1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The black nodes are M and B.

2.  **Identify direct connections from M:**
    *   M is connected to J.
    *   M is connected to O.
    *   M is connected to L.
    These are the only three edges originating from M.

3.  **Identify direct connections to B:**
    *   B is connected to N.
    *   B is connected to C.
    *   B is connected to E.
    *   B is connected to O.
    These are the only four edges directly connected to B.

4.  **Consider isolating one of the target nodes:**
    *   To isolate node M, all edges connected to it must be removed. These are (M, J), (M, O), and (M, L). Removing these 3 edges would completely disconnect M from the rest of the graph, and thus from B. This is a potential cut set of size 3: `[(M, J), (M, O), (M, L)]`.
    *   To isolate node B, all edges connected to it must be removed. These are (B, N), (B, C), (B, E), and (B, O). Removing these 4 edges would completely disconnect B from the rest of the graph, and thus from M. This is a potential cut set of size 4.

5.  **Determine the upper bound for the minimum cut:** Since we found a cut set of size 3, the minimum number of edges to remove is at most 3.

6.  **Attempt to find a cut set of size 1 or 2:**
    *   **Size 1:** A single edge removal cannot disconnect M and B. For example, removing (M, O) still leaves paths like M-J-I-C-B and M-L-E-B. Removing (O, B) still leaves paths like M-O-N-B, M-J-I-C-B, and M-L-E-B. Therefore, the minimum cut is greater than 1.

    *   **Size 2:** We need to systematically check if any two edges can disconnect M and B.
        *   **Case A: Removing two edges connected to M.**
            *   If (M, J) and (M, O) are removed: Path M-L-E-B still exists.
            *   If (M, J) and (M, L) are removed: Path M-O-B still exists.
            *   If (M, O) and (M, L) are removed: Path M-J-I-C-B still exists.
            Therefore, no two edges connected to M can disconnect M and B.

        *   **Case B: Removing two edges connected to B.**
            *   If (B, N) and (B, C) are removed: Paths M-O-B and M-L-E-B still exist.
            *   If (B, N) and (B, E) are removed: Paths M-O-B and M-J-I-C-B still exist.
            *   If (B, N) and (B, O) are removed: Paths M-L-E-B and M-J-I-C-B still exist.
            *   If (B, C) and (B, E) are removed: Paths M-O-B and M-O-N-B still exist.
            *   If (B, C) and (B, O) are removed: Paths M-L-E-B and M-O-N-B still exist.
            *   If (B, E) and (B, O) are removed: Paths M-J-I-C-B and M-O-N-B still exist.
            Therefore, no two edges connected to B can disconnect M and B.

        *   **Case C: Removing one edge connected to M and one edge connected to B (or two "middle" edges).**
            Let's consider three distinct paths from M to B:
            1.  P1: M - O - B
            2.  P2: M - L - E - B
            3.  P3: M - J - I - C - B

            These three paths are edge-disjoint:
            *   P1 uses edges (M, O) and (O, B).
            *   P2 uses edges (M, L), (L, E), and (E, B).
            *   P3 uses edges (M, J), (J, I), (I, C), and (C, B).

            Since these three paths are completely edge-disjoint, by Menger's Theorem, at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect M and B. If we remove any two edges, at least one of these three paths will remain intact. For example:
            *   If we remove (M, O) and (E, B): P1 is broken, P2 is broken, but P3 (M-J-I-C-B) is still active.
            *   If we remove (M, J) and (O, B): P1 is broken, P3 is broken, but P2 (M-L-E-B) is still active.
            *   If we remove (M, L) and (C, B): P2 is broken, P3 is broken, but P1 (M-O-B) is still active.

7.  **Conclusion:** Since we have identified 3 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum cut must be at least 3. We have also found a cut set of size 3 by removing all edges connected to M: `[(M, J), (M, O), (M, L)]`. This confirms that 3 is the minimum number of edges.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(M, J), (M, O), (M, L)]

1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are J and L.
2.  **Analyze connections to the target nodes:**
    *   Node L is directly connected only to nodes M and E.
    *   Node J is directly connected to nodes D, P, M, and I.
3.  **Identify critical choke points:** Since L is only connected to M and E, any path from J to L *must* pass through either M or E. This means that to disconnect J and L, we must remove at least one node from the set {M, E}. This implies that the minimum number of nodes to remove is at least 1.
4.  **Test for a single-node cut:**
    *   If we remove node M: The path J-M-L is broken. However, paths like J-D-P-O-N-B-E-L still exist, connecting J to L through E. So, removing M alone is not sufficient.
    *   If we remove node E: All paths that go through E to reach L are broken. However, the path J-M-L still exists. So, removing E alone is not sufficient.
    *   If we remove any other single node (e.g., B, F, O, I, H, C, D, P, Y, N, X, K, G): The path J-M-L would still exist, and at least one path through E (e.g., J-D-P-O-N-B-E-L or J-I-H-X-K-F-E-L) would also still exist. Therefore, no single node removal is sufficient.
5.  **Conclusion for single-node cut:** Since no single node can disconnect J and L, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2.
6.  **Test for a two-node cut:** Based on step 3, we know we must remove at least one of M or E.
    *   **Option A: Remove M and E.**
        *   If M is removed, the path J-M-L is broken.
        *   If E is removed, all paths ending in E-L are broken.
        *   With both M and E removed, L has no remaining connections, thus J and L are completely disconnected.
        *   Neither M nor E are the target nodes J or L.
        *   This forms a valid cut set: [M, E].
    *   **Option B: Remove M and another node (e.g., B or F).**
        *   If M is removed, all remaining paths must go through E. We need to disconnect J from E.
        *   Paths from J to E:
            *   Paths starting J-D-P or J-P: J-D/P-O-N-B-E and J-D/P-O-Y-N-B-E. These all pass through B.
            *   Paths starting J-I: J-I-H-C-B-E, J-I-C-B-E, J-I-C-O-N-B-E, J-I-C-O-Y-N-B-E (all pass through B). Also, J-I-H-X-K-F-E, J-I-H-G-X-K-F-E, J-I-H-G-K-F-E (all pass through F).
        *   Therefore, if M is removed, all remaining paths from J to E pass through either B or F. To disconnect J from E, we would need to remove both B and F. This would result in a cut set of [M, B, F], which has a size of 3.
7.  **Compare cut sets:** We found a cut set of size 2 ([M, E]) and a cut set of size 3 ([M, B, F]). Since we determined that the minimum cut set size must be at least 2, the cut set [M, E] is the minimum.

**Category 1: Paths starting with H-I**
1.  H - I - C - B
2.  H - I - P - O - B
3.  H - I - P - O - N - B
4.  H - I - J - M - O - B
5.  H - I - J - M - L - E - B

**Category 2: Paths starting with H-G**
6.  H - G - X - F - E - B
7.  H - G - F - E - B

**Category 3: Paths starting with H-X**
8.  H - X - F - E - B
9.  H - X - K - F - E - B

Now, I need to find a minimum set of edges whose removal breaks all these paths.

**Attempting to find a cut of size 1:**
*   There is no single edge that directly connects H and B.
*   If I remove any single edge, for example (H, I), paths from H-G and H-X would still exist.
*   If I remove any single edge, for example (F, E), paths from H-I would still exist (e.g., H-I-C-B).
Therefore, a cut of size 1 is not possible. The minimum number of edges must be at least 2.

**Attempting to find a cut of size 2:**
Let's look for "bottleneck" edges that are common to multiple paths.

*   **Observation 1:** All paths in Category 2 (H-G-...) and Category 3 (H-X-...) eventually converge and pass through the edge (F, E) to reach node B via node E.
    *   Paths H-G-X-F-E-B, H-G-F-E-B, H-X-F-E-B, H-X-K-F-E-B all use the edge (F, E).
    *   Therefore, removing the edge **(F, E)** will disconnect all paths in Category 2 and Category 3 from B.

*   **Observation 2:** After removing (F, E), the only remaining paths are those in Category 1 (starting with H-I). To disconnect these, I need to cut at least one edge from each of them. The most efficient way to cut all paths originating from H via I is to remove the initial edge **(H, I)**.
    *   Removing (H, I) will break all paths: H-I-C-B, H-I-P-O-B, H-I-P-O-N-B, H-I-J-M-O-B, H-I-J-M-L-E-B.

**Proposed Cut Set:** [(H, I), (F, E)]

**Verification:**
1.  **Remove (H, I):** All paths starting with H-I are immediately broken. This includes all paths in Category 1.
2.  **Remove (F, E):** All paths starting with H-G or H-X (Category 2 and 3) are broken because they all rely on the (F, E) edge to reach B.
Since all paths from H to B are broken by removing these two edges, and we've established that a cut of size 1 is not possible, this cut set of size 2 is the minimum.

Final Answer:

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are B and M. The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect B from M.

First, I identify all direct connections to the target node M. Node M is connected to E, L, and J.
Therefore, any path from B to M must pass through one of these three nodes (E, L, or J) and then use the direct edge to M.

Let's list the edges directly connected to M:
1.  (E, M)
2.  (L, M)
3.  (J, M)

If we remove all three of these edges, M becomes isolated from the rest of the graph, and thus from B. This would be a cut set of size 3: [(E, M), (L, M), (J, M)]. This is a valid cut set.

Now, I need to determine if a smaller cut set (i.e., 1 or 2 edges) can achieve the same disconnection.

**Can 1 edge disconnect B and M?**
No, because M has three distinct connections. Removing one edge would leave at least two other paths to M. For example, removing (E, M) would leave paths like B-I-J-M and B-H-E-L-M.

**Can 2 edges disconnect B and M?**
Let's test all combinations of removing two edges from the set of edges directly connected to M:
1.  **Remove (E, M) and (L, M):**
    *   A path from B to M still exists through J. For example, B - I - J - M.
    *   Therefore, this set of 2 edges is not sufficient.

2.  **Remove (E, M) and (J, M):**
    *   A path from B to M still exists through L. For example, B - H - E - L - M.
    *   Therefore, this set of 2 edges is not sufficient.

3.  **Remove (L, M) and (J, M):**
    *   A path from B to M still exists through E. For example, B - I - E - M.
    *   Therefore, this set of 2 edges is not sufficient.

Since removing any two of the edges directly connected to M does not disconnect B from M, it implies that if a cut of size 2 exists, at least one of the removed edges must be *not* directly connected to M.

Let's consider the case where two edges, e1 and e2, are removed, and at least one of them is not directly connected to M.
If (E, M), (L, M), and (J, M) are all still present, then to disconnect B from M, we must disconnect B from E, B from L, and B from J.

Let's list some paths from B to E, L, and J:
*   **Paths from B to E:**
    *   B - I - E
    *   B - H - E
    *   B - H - G - F - E
*   **Paths from B to L:**
    *   B - I - E - L
    *   B - H - E - L
    *   B - H - G - F - E - L
*   **Paths from B to J:**
    *   B - I - J
    *   B - I - P - J
    *   B - C - O - P - J

Let's try to remove two edges that are not directly connected to M and see if any path remains:
1.  **Remove (I, E) and (H, E):**
    *   This cuts paths from B to E via I and H.
    *   However, paths from B to J are still active, e.g., B - I - J - M.
    *   Therefore, this set of 2 edges is not sufficient.

2.  **Remove (I, J) and (P, J):**
    *   This cuts paths from B to J via I and P.
    *   However, paths from B to E are still active, e.g., B - I - E - M.
    *   Therefore, this set of 2 edges is not sufficient.

3.  **Remove (E, L) and (I, J):**
    *   This cuts paths to L via E and paths to J via I.
    *   However, paths from B to E are still active, e.g., B - H - E - M.
    *   Therefore, this set of 2 edges is not sufficient.

Based on this analysis, it is not possible to disconnect B from M by removing only 2 edges. The three distinct "entry points" to M (E, L, J) require at least one cut for each, or a common cut that affects all three. The most direct way to cut all paths is to remove the edges directly connecting to M.

Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(E, M), (L, M), (J, M)]

    *   If we remove all edges connected to B: (E, B), (N, B), (C, B), (H, B). This would isolate B from the rest of the graph, thus disconnecting it from M. This cut set has 4 edges.
    *   The cut set [(M, L), (M, E), (M, J)] is a candidate for a minimal cut of size 3.

3.  **Apply Menger's Theorem (finding edge-disjoint paths):** To confirm if 3 is indeed the minimum, I will try to find 3 edge-disjoint paths between M and B. If I can find 3 such paths, then by Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove must be at least 3.

    Let's identify three edge-disjoint paths:
    *   **Path 1:** M - E - B
        Edges: (M, E), (E, B)

    *   **Path 2:** M - J - P - I - C - B
        Edges: (M, J), (J, P), (P, I), (I, C), (C, B)

    *   **Path 3:** M - L - E - F - K - G - H - B
        Edges: (M, L), (L, E), (E, F), (F, K), (K, G), (G, H), (H, B)

    Let's verify if these paths are edge-disjoint:
    *   Edges in Path 1: {(M, E), (E, B)}
    *   Edges in Path 2: {(M, J), (J, P), (P, I), (I, C), (C, B)}
    *   Edges in Path 3: {(M, L), (L, E), (E, F), (F, K), (K, G), (G, H), (H, B)}

    Upon inspection, all edges listed for Path 1, Path 2, and Path 3 are unique and do not overlap. For example, Path 1 uses (M, E) while Path 3 uses (M, L); Path 1 uses (E, B) while Path 2 uses (C, B) and Path 3 uses (H, B). Path 1 and Path 3 both involve node E, but they use different edges connected to E ((M,E) vs (L,E) and (E,B) vs (E,F)).

    Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between M and B, the minimum number of edges required to disconnect them is at least 3.

4.  **Confirm the minimal cut set:**
    I previously identified the cut set [(M, L), (M, E), (M, J)] which has a size of 3.
    If these three edges are removed, node M becomes isolated from the rest of the graph. Consequently, no path can exist from M to B.

5.  **Conclusion:** Since the minimum number of edges required is at least 3 (from Menger's Theorem) and I have found a cut set of size 3, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Step-by-step reasoning:
1.  **Identify Target Nodes:** The two black nodes are G and N. The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect G from N.

2.  **Initial Assessment of Connectivity:**
    *   Node G is connected to E, F, H, K.
    *   Node N is connected to B, C, Y.
    *   The graph appears to have multiple paths between G and N, suggesting a cut of size greater than 1.

3.  **Strategy: Identify Bottleneck Edges/Channels:**
    I will look for "bottleneck" edges that, if removed, would sever major pathways from the "G-side" of the graph to the "N-side". I can conceptualize a cut that separates G and its immediate cluster from N and its immediate cluster.

    Let's consider the main "channels" through which paths from G can reach N:
    *   **Channel 1 (via H):** Paths starting G → H → ... (e.g., G-H-I-C-N, G-H-I-O-Y-N). The edge (G, H) is a critical link for this channel.
    *   **Channel 2 (via E to B):** Paths starting G → E → B → N (e.g., G-E-B-N, G-F-E-B-N, G-K-X-F-E-B-N). The edge (E, B) is a critical link for this channel.
    *   **Channel 3 (via M to J):** Paths starting G → E → L → M → J → ... (e.g., G-E-L-M-J-P-I-C-N, G-F-E-L-M-J-P-O-Y-N). The edge (M, J) is a critical link for this channel.

4.  **Propose a Cut Set:**
    Based on the identified channels, I propose the following set of edges to remove:
    *   (G, H) - to cut off the H-channel.
    *   (E, B) - to cut off the direct E-B path to N.
    *   (M, J) - to cut off the M-J path that leads to the N-side.

    This cut set is: `[(G, H), (E, B), (M, J)]`. The size of this set is 3.

5.  **Verify Disconnection:**
    Let's check if removing these three edges disconnects G from N:
    *   If (G, H) is removed, no path can start from G through H.
    *   If (E, B) is removed, no path can reach B from E. Since B is directly connected to N, this cuts a significant route.
    *   If (M, J) is removed, no path can reach J from M. J is a central hub for many paths leading to I, P, O, C, Y, and eventually N.

    Let's trace all possible paths from G:
    *   **Paths starting G-H...:** These are all blocked by removing (G, H).
    *   **Paths starting G-E...:**
        *   G-E-B-N: Blocked by removing (E, B).
        *   G-E-L-M-J...: Blocked by removing (M, J).
    *   **Paths starting G-F...:**
        *   G-F-E-B-N: Blocked by removing (E, B).
        *   G-F-E-L-M-J...: Blocked by removing (M, J).
    *   **Paths starting G-K...:**
        *   G-K-X-F-E-B-N: Blocked by removing (E, B).
        *   G-K-X-F-E-L-M-J...: Blocked by removing (M, J).

    All identified paths from G to N are indeed broken by the removal of these three edges.

6.  **Prove Minimality (using Menger's Theorem intuition):**
    To prove that 3 is the minimum, I need to find at least 3 edge-disjoint paths between G and N. If such paths exist, then by Menger's theorem, at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect G from N.

    Let's find three edge-disjoint paths:
    *   **Path 1:** G - H - I - C - N
        Edges: (G, H), (H, I), (I, C), (C, N)
    *   **Path 2:** G - E - B - N
        Edges: (G, E), (E, B), (B, N)
    *   **Path 3:** G - F - E - L - M - J - P - O - Y - N
        Edges: (G, F), (F, E), (E, L), (L, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, O), (O, Y), (Y, N)

    Let's check for edge-disjointness:
    *   (G, H) is unique to Path 1.
    *   (E, B) is unique to Path 2.
    *   (M, J) is unique to Path 3.
    *   No other edges are shared between these three paths. For example, Path 1 uses (H, I) while Path 2 uses (G, E) and Path 3 uses (G, F). Path 2 uses (B, N) while Path 1 uses (C, N) and Path 3 uses (Y, N).

    Since I have found three edge-disjoint paths between G and N, the minimum number of edges to remove must be at least 3. As I have found a cut set of size 3 that disconnects G and N, this is indeed the minimum.

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are N and G. I need to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect N from G.

2.  **Analyze connections from N:**
    *   N is connected to Y, O, C, B.

3.  **Analyze connections to G:**
    *   G is connected to K, X, H.

4.  **Identify major pathways/bottlenecks:**
    All paths from N to G must eventually pass through one of G's neighbors (K, X, H). Let's examine how these neighbors are connected to the rest of the graph (and thus, to N).
    *   **H** is connected to B, I, and K.
    *   **K** is connected to F and H.
    *   **X** is connected to F.

    This means any path to G must pass through H, or through K (which connects to F or H), or through X (which connects to F).
    Essentially, all paths to G must pass through H or F (since F connects to both K and X).

5.  **Test for a single-node cut:**
    *   If I remove H:
        *   Paths N -> ... -> H -> G are broken.
        *   However, paths N -> ... -> F -> K -> G and N -> ... -> F -> X -> G still exist. For example, N -> B -> E -> F -> K -> G. So, H alone is not enough.
    *   If I remove F:
        *   Paths N -> ... -> F -> K -> G and N -> ... -> F -> X -> G are broken.
        *   However, paths N -> ... -> H -> G still exist. For example, N -> B -> H -> G. So, F alone is not enough.
    *   If I remove any other node (B, C, O, Y, I, P, D, J, M, L, E, K, X), there will always be alternative paths. For example, removing B leaves paths through C, O, Y. Removing I leaves paths through B. Removing E leaves paths through H.
    Therefore, a single node removal is not sufficient. The minimum number of nodes to remove is at least 2.

6.  **Test for a two-node cut:**
    Based on the observation that all paths to G must pass through H or F, let's try removing both H and F.
    *   **Remove H:**
        *   All direct paths to G via H are severed (e.g., N -> B -> H -> G, N -> C -> I -> H -> G).
        *   Node K is connected to H and F. With H removed, K can only connect to F.
        *   Node X is connected only to F.
        *   After removing H, any remaining path to G must go through K (via F) or X (via F). This means all remaining paths must pass through F.
    *   **Now, remove F (in addition to H):**
        *   All paths that were going through F to K (e.g., N -> B -> E -> F -> K -> G) are now broken.
        *   All paths that were going through F to X (e.g., N -> B -> E -> F -> X -> G) are now broken.
        *   Since H is removed, K's connection to H is gone. Since F is removed, K's connection to F is gone. Thus, K is isolated from the N-side.
        *   Since F is removed, X's connection to F is gone. Thus, X is isolated from the N-side.
        *   With H, K, and X all disconnected from the N-side, G is completely disconnected from N.

7.  **Conclusion:** Removing nodes H and F successfully disconnects N from G. Since a single-node cut was not possible, a cut set of size 2 is the minimum.

Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are F and J. The task is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect F from J.

First, let's identify the immediate neighbors of F and J:
*   Neighbors of F: K, X, G, E
*   Neighbors of J: P, I, D, M

Let's try to find a cut set of 2 edges.
Consider the main "branches" originating from F:
1.  Paths through K, X, G.
2.  Paths through E.

All paths from F that go through K, X, or G must eventually pass through the edge (G, H) to reach the "right" side of the graph (H, I, B, C, etc.) and eventually J. There are no other connections from K, X, or G to the rest of the graph except through H via (G,H).
So, if we remove the edge (G, H), all paths from F through K, X, G are broken.

Now, we need to consider paths from F through E.
These paths can go in several directions:
*   F - E - M - J
*   F - E - L - M - J
*   F - E - B - H - I - J
*   F - E - B - C - I - J
*   F - E - B - C - O - P - J
*   F - E - B - N - C - I - J
*   F - E - B - N - O - P - J

If we choose a second edge to remove, for example (E, B), this would cut all paths from F that go through E and then B.
So, if we remove [(G, H), (E, B)]:
1.  (G, H) breaks all paths F -> {K,X,G} -> ... -> J.
2.  (E, B) breaks all paths F -> E -> B -> ... -> J.

However, after removing (G, H) and (E, B), there are still paths remaining:
*   F - E - M - J (still exists)
*   F - E - L - M - J (still exists)

Since these paths still exist, a cut set of 2 edges is not sufficient to disconnect F and J. Therefore, the minimum number of edges must be at least 3.

Now, let's try to find a cut set of 3 edges.
We know that (G, H) is a critical edge for the F-{K,X,G} branch, and (E, B) is critical for the F-E-B branch.
Let's keep these two edges in our potential cut set: [(G, H), (E, B)].
We still need to cut the paths F - E - M - J and F - E - L - M - J.
Both of these paths converge on M and then go to J via (M, J).
If we add (M, J) to our cut set, it will break both of these remaining paths.

Let's verify the proposed cut set: [(G, H), (E, B), (M, J)].

1.  **Remove (G, H):**
    *   All paths from F that go through K, X, or G are disconnected from the rest of the graph (H, I, B, etc.), and thus from J.
    *   Examples: F-G-H-I-J, F-K-G-H-I-J, F-X-G-H-I-J are all broken.

2.  **Remove (E, B):**
    *   All paths from F that go through E and then B are disconnected from the rest of the graph (H, C, N, O, P), and thus from J.
    *   Examples: F-E-B-H-I-J, F-E-B-C-I-J, F-E-B-C-O-P-J, F-E-B-N-O-P-J are all broken.

3.  **Remove (M, J):**
    *   All paths from F that go through M to J are disconnected.
    *   Examples: F-E-M-J, F-E-L-M-J are both broken.

After removing these three edges, there are no remaining paths from F to J.
Since we have established that 2 edges are not sufficient, and 3 edges are sufficient, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

The target nodes are E and J. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect E from J. I will use Menger's theorem, which states that the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them.

First, I identify potential paths between E and J. I will look for paths that do not share any edges.

1.  **Path 1:** E - M - J
    Edges: (E, M), (M, J)

2.  **Path 2:** E - I - J
    Edges: (E, I), (I, J)

3.  **Path 3:** E - B - C - O - P - J
    Edges: (E, B), (B, C), (C, O), (O, P), (P, J)

Now, I verify if these three paths are edge-disjoint:
*   Path 1 uses edges (E, M) and (M, J).
*   Path 2 uses edges (E, I) and (I, J).
*   Path 3 uses edges (E, B), (B, C), (C, O), (O, P), and (P, J).

Comparing the edge sets, I can confirm that no edge is shared between any two of these three paths. Therefore, these are three edge-disjoint paths.

According to Menger's theorem, since there are at least 3 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum number of edges required to disconnect E from J must be at least 3.

Next, I need to find a cut set of 3 edges that disconnects E from J. A straightforward approach is to cut all the edges directly connecting J to the "main" pathways from E. The nodes M, I, and P are the primary gateways to J from E's side.

Consider the following cut set:
*   (J, M) - This edge cuts Path 1 (E - M - J) and any other path through M to J (e.g., E - L - M - J).
*   (J, I) - This edge cuts Path 2 (E - I - J) and any other path through I to J (e.g., E - B - I - J, E - F - G - H - B - I - J).
*   (J, P) - This edge cuts Path 3 (E - B - C - O - P - J) and any other path through P to J (e.g., E - I - P - J, E - I - C - O - P - J).

Let's verify that removing these three edges completely disconnects E from J:
After removing (J, M), (J, I), and (J, P), node J is only connected to node D.
Now, I need to check if there is any path from E to D.
The neighbors of E are F, L, M, I, B.
The neighbors of D are J and P.
Since (J, P) is removed, D is only connected to J.
None of E's neighbors (F, L, M, I, B) are D, and there are no paths from E to D through any other nodes in the graph. Therefore, E cannot reach D.
Since J is only connected to D, and E cannot reach D, E is completely disconnected from J.

This cut set has 3 edges, and we've established that at least 3 edges are required. Thus, this is a minimal cut set.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(J, M), (J, I), (J, P)]

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The goal is to find the minimum number of nodes to remove to disconnect the black nodes H and E, without removing H or E themselves.

I will use Menger's Theorem, which states that the maximum number of node-disjoint paths between two nodes is equal to the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect them.

I need to find node-disjoint paths from H to E. Node-disjoint means that the paths share no common intermediate nodes (nodes other than H and E).

Let's identify paths from H to E:

1.  **Path 1: H - I - J - M - L - E**
    *   Intermediate nodes: {I, J, M, L}

2.  **Path 2: H - C - B - E**
    *   Intermediate nodes: {C, B}

3.  **Path 3: H - X - K - F - E**
    *   Intermediate nodes: {X, K, F}

Let's check if these three paths are node-disjoint:
*   The set of intermediate nodes for Path 1 is {I, J, M, L}.
*   The set of intermediate nodes for Path 2 is {C, B}.
*   The set of intermediate nodes for Path 3 is {X, K, F}.

These three sets of intermediate nodes are mutually exclusive (they do not share any common nodes). Therefore, these three paths are node-disjoint.

Since I have found three node-disjoint paths between H and E, according to Menger's Theorem, at least 3 nodes must be removed to disconnect H from E.

Now, I need to find a cut set of size 3.
Consider the nodes directly connected to E: B, F, L.
If we remove these three nodes (B, F, L), E will have no remaining connections to the rest of the graph. Consequently, no path can exist from H to E.
The nodes B, F, and L are not H or E, so their removal is allowed.

Thus, a cut set of size 3 is [B, F, L].
Since we established that at least 3 nodes are required, and we found a cut set of size 3, this is the minimum number of nodes.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [B, F, L]

The target nodes are G and I. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between G and I.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their direct connections:**
    *   Node G is connected to: X, H, F.
    *   Node I is connected to: H, C, J.

2.  **Consider a simple strategy: Isolate one of the target nodes.**
    *   To isolate node I, all edges directly connected to it must be removed. These edges are (H,I), (C,I), and (J,I).
    *   If these three edges are removed, node I becomes an isolated node, meaning no path can reach it from G or any other node in the graph.
    *   This forms a cut set of size 3: [(H,I), (C,I), (J,I)].

3.  **Determine if a smaller cut set (e.g., size 2) is possible.**
    *   Let's assume a cut set of size 2 exists. This means we could remove only two edges and disconnect G from I.
    *   Consider removing any two edges directly connected to I.
        *   If we remove (H,I) and (C,I), the edge (J,I) remains. We need to check if there's still a path from G to J.
            *   Path 1: G - F - E - M - J. This path exists.
            *   Path 2: G - X - K - M - J. This path exists.
            *   Path 3: G - X - F - E - M - J. This path exists.
            Since paths from G to J exist, and (J,I) is still present, a path from G to I (e.g., G - F - E - M - J - I) would still exist. Therefore, removing (H,I) and (C,I) is not sufficient.
        *   By symmetry, removing any other pair of edges incident to I (e.g., (H,I) and (J,I), or (C,I) and (J,I)) would leave one edge incident to I, and there are paths from G to the node connected by that remaining edge (e.g., G to C, or G to H).
    *   This demonstrates that removing any two edges directly connected to I is not sufficient to disconnect G from I.
    *   Similarly, removing any two edges directly connected to G would leave one edge, and there are paths from that remaining neighbor of G to I. For example, if (G,X) and (G,F) are removed, (G,H) remains, and G-H-I is a direct path.

4.  **Conclusion on minimum size:**
    *   Since we've shown that no cut set of size 2 can disconnect G from I, the minimum number of edges to remove must be at least 3.
    *   We have already identified a cut set of size 3: [(H,I), (C,I), (J,I)]. This set successfully disconnects I from the rest of the graph, thus ensuring no path exists between G and I.

Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(H, I), (C, I), (J, I)]

        *   **Bottleneck 1: Node P.** If P is removed, all paths that directly use P (F-P-J, F-P-D-J) are broken. Also, paths like F-G-H-I-P-J, F-G-H-C-N-O-P-J, and F-G-H-C-N-Y-O-P-J are broken because they rely on P.

    *   Since there are at least two distinct "channels" (one through P, and another through G-H-C-B-E-M), we need to remove at least one node from each channel to disconnect F and J.
    *   Removing P breaks all paths that go through P.
    *   After removing P, the only remaining paths from F to J are those that go through F -> G -> H -> C -> B -> E -> M -> J (and its variant via L).
    *   To break these remaining paths, we need to remove one node from the set {G, H, C, B, E, M}.
    *   Therefore, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 1 (P) + 1 (from {G, H, C, B, E, M}) = 2.

6.  **Select a minimal cut set:**
    *   A valid cut set of size 2 would be {P, G}.
    *   Another valid cut set of size 2 would be {P, M}.
