--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are E and H. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect E and H. I will use Menger's Theorem, which states that the minimum number of edges to remove (minimum cut) is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between the two nodes.

First, I identify all possible paths between E and H. I will then try to find the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths.

**Paths from E to H:**

1.  **Direct Path:** E - H
    *   Edges: (E, H)

2.  **Paths through F:**
    *   E - F - H
    *   Edges: (E, F), (F, H)

3.  **Paths through G:**
    *   E - G - H
    *   Edges: (E, G), (G, H)

4.  **Paths through the "right side" (L, M, J, I, P, C, B, N, O, Y):**
    *   E can connect to L or M.
    *   H can connect to I or B.

    Let's try to find a path from E through L to I, and then to H:
    *   E - L - M - J - I - H
    *   Edges: (E, L), (L, M), (M, J), (J, I), (I, H)

Now, let's check if these four paths are edge-disjoint:
*   **Path 1 (P1):** E - H. Edges: {(E, H)}
*   **Path 2 (P2):** E - F - H. Edges: {(E, F), (F, H)}
*   **Path 3 (P3):** E - G - H. Edges: {(E, G), (G, H)}
*   **Path 4 (P4):** E - L - M - J - I - H. Edges: {(E, L), (L, M), (M, J), (J, I), (I, H)}

Let's examine the edges used by each path:
*   P1 uses (E, H).
*   P2 uses (E, F) and (F, H). These are distinct from P1's edge.
*   P3 uses (E, G) and (G, H). These are distinct from P1 and P2's edges.
*   P4 uses (E, L), (L, M), (M, J), (J, I), (I, H). These are distinct from P1, P2, and P3's edges.

Since I have found 4 paths that are completely edge-disjoint, by Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect E and H is at least 4.

Next, I need to find a cut set of 4 edges that, when removed, disconnects E and H.

Consider the following set of 4 edges:
1.  (E, H) - This cuts the direct path.
2.  (F, H) - This cuts all paths from E to H that go through F (e.g., E-F-H, E-F-K-G-H, E-F-X-G-H).
3.  (G, H) - This cuts all paths from E to H that go through G (e.g., E-G-H, E-G-K-F-H, E-G-X-F-H).
4.  (M, J) - This edge is a critical bottleneck for all paths from E to H that go through the "right side" of the graph (via L or M).

Let's verify if removing these 4 edges disconnects E and H:
*   **Path 1 (E-H):** Broken by removing (E, H).
*   **Paths through F:** Any path like E-...-F-H is broken by removing (F, H).
*   **Paths through G:** Any path like E-...-G-H is broken by removing (G, H).
*   **Paths through L/M to I/B:**
    *   E connects to L and M.
    *   From L, the only way to reach the central "right side" cluster (J, I, P, C, B, N, O, Y) is via M (L-M).
    *   From M, the only way to reach the central "right side" cluster is via J (M-J).
    *   Since (M, J) is removed, no path from E (via L or M) can reach J.
    *   Nodes I, P, D, O are all directly connected to J. Since J is unreachable from E, these nodes are also unreachable from E.
    *   Nodes C, B, N, Y are connected to I, P, O, J. Since I, P, O, J are unreachable from E, C, B, N, Y are also unreachable from E.
    *   Since H is connected to I and B (on the right side), and both I and B are unreachable from E, H cannot be reached from E via the right side.

Therefore, removing the edges [(E, H), (F, H), (G, H), (M, J)] successfully disconnects E and H.
The size of this cut set is 4.

Since I found 4 edge-disjoint paths and a cut set of size 4, the minimum number of edges to remove is 4.

Model: gemini-2.5-flash

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  Identify the two black nodes: E and I.
2.  The goal is to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect E and I. The black nodes themselves cannot be removed.
3.  First, let's identify the direct neighbors of E: L, M, F, G, B.
4.  Next, identify the direct neighbors of I: P, H, C.
5.  Any path from E to I must pass through one of E's neighbors and one of I's neighbors.

Let's consider the nodes directly connected to I: P, H, C. If we remove all three of these nodes, no path can reach I. Therefore, {P, H, C} is a cut set of size 3. This establishes an upper bound for the minimum number of nodes to remove.

Now, let's try to determine if a cut set of size 1 or 2 is possible.

**Attempt to find a cut set of size 1:**
A single node removal would mean that all paths from E to I pass through that one node.
*   Consider H: Paths like E-B-C-I or E-M-J-P-I would still exist.
*   Consider C: Paths like E-G-H-I or E-M-J-P-I would still exist.
*   Consider P: Paths like E-G-H-I or E-B-C-I would still exist.
*   Consider J: Paths like E-G-H-I or E-B-C-I would still exist.
*   Consider B: Paths like E-G-H-I or E-M-J-P-I would still exist.
*   Consider G: Paths like E-B-H-I or E-M-J-P-I would still exist.
No single node can disconnect E and I.

**Attempt to find a cut set of size 2:**
This requires finding two nodes, say X and Y, such that every path from E to I passes through X or Y.

Let's analyze the paths from E to I by considering the "branches" originating from E's neighbors:
*   **Branch 1 (via L/M to J):** Paths like E-L-M-J-P-I, E-L-M-J-O-C-I, E-M-J-P-I, E-M-J-O-C-I. These paths all go through J.
*   **Branch 2 (via F/G to H):** Paths like E-F-X-K-G-H-I, E-F-K-G-H-I, E-F-G-H-I, E-G-H-I. These paths all go through G.
*   **Branch 3 (via B to H/C/N):** Paths like E-B-H-I, E-B-C-I, E-B-N-C-I, E-B-N-Y-O-C-I, E-B-N-Y-O-P-I, E-B-C-O-P-I. These paths all go through B.

Now, let's test combinations of two nodes from these critical "branching" nodes {J, G, B}:
1.  **Remove J and G:**
    *   Paths through J are broken (Branch 1).
    *   Paths through G are broken (Branch 2).
    *   However, paths through B (Branch 3) still exist, e.g., E-B-H-I. So, {J, G} is not a cut set.
2.  **Remove J and B:**
    *   Paths through J are broken (Branch 1).
    *   Paths through B are broken (Branch 3).
    *   However, paths through G (Branch 2) still exist, e.g., E-G-H-I. So, {J, B} is not a cut set.
3.  **Remove G and B:**
    *   Paths through G are broken (Branch 2).
    *   Paths through B are broken (Branch 3).
    *   However, paths through J (Branch 1) still exist, e.g., E-M-J-P-I. So, {G, B} is not a cut set.

Since no pair of these critical nodes can disconnect E and I, a cut set of size 2 is not possible.

**Minimum cut set of size 3:**
As established earlier, the set of nodes {P, H, C} (all direct neighbors of I) forms a cut set of size 3.
Alternatively, the set of nodes {J, G, B} also forms a cut set of size 3:
*   Removing J disconnects all paths from E via L or M.
*   Removing G disconnects all paths from E via F or G.
*   Removing B disconnects all paths from E via B.
Since all paths from E must start through one of its neighbors (L, M, F, G, B), and these three nodes (J, G, B) effectively block all these initial branches leading to I, E and I become completely disconnected.

Therefore, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [J, G, B] (or [P, H, C])

    *   To disconnect F, we can remove all edges connected to it: (K, F), (X, F), (G, F), (E, F). This cut set has 4 edges.
    *   Since we are looking for the *minimum* number of edges, a cut of size 3 is better than a cut of size 4. This means the minimum cut is at most 3.

3.  **Check if a cut of size 1 is possible.**
    *   If we remove only one edge, for example (J, D), paths from F to D via P (e.g., F-K-M-J-P-D) or via Y (e.g., F-E-B-C-O-Y-D) would still exist.
    *   Similarly, removing (P, D) would leave paths via J and Y.
    *   Removing (Y, D) would leave paths via J and P.
    *   Therefore, a single edge removal is not sufficient to disconnect F from D.

4.  **Check if a cut of size 2 is possible.**

        *   Paths to J come from M and I.
        *   Paths to P come from I, J, and O.
        *   Paths to Y come from O.

        *   Let's systematically test combinations of two edges from this set to see if they can disconnect F from D.
            *   If we remove (M, J) and (I, J): Paths via I to P (e.g., F-X-G-H-I-P-D) and paths via O to P or Y (e.g., F-E-B-C-O-P-D, F-E-B-C-O-Y-D) would still exist.

    *   Therefore, a cut of size 2 is not sufficient.

    *   **Paths starting M -> F:**

        *   P10: M - J - P - I - H - G - B
        *   P11: M - J - P - I - H - B

3.  **Apply Menger's Theorem (Vertex-Disjoint Paths):** To find the minimum vertex cut, I look for the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between M and B (excluding M and B themselves).
    *   **Path 1 (P1'):** M - L - E - B (Intermediate nodes: L, E)
    *   **Path 2 (P2'):** M - F - K - G - B (Intermediate nodes: F, K, G)

        *   These nodes (J, P, I, H) are distinct from (L, E) and (F, K, G).

    Since I found three vertex-disjoint paths between M and B, by Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect M and B is at least 3.

4.  **Find a Cut Set of Size 3:** Now I need to find a set of 3 nodes whose removal disconnects M and B.
    Let's consider the nodes that act as "gateways" for the different path groups:
    *   Paths P1-P4 (via L or F, then E): All these paths pass through E.
    *   Paths P5-P6 (via F, K): These paths pass through G.
    *   Paths P7-P11 (via J): All these paths pass through P.

    Consider removing the nodes {E, G, P}.
    *   If **E** is removed: Paths P1, P2, P3, P4 are broken.
    *   If **G** is removed: Paths P2, P4, P5, P6 are broken. (Note: P2 and P4 are already broken by E, but G also covers the K-G-B paths).
    *   If **P** is removed: Paths P7, P8, P9, P10, P11 are broken.

    Let's verify if M is disconnected from B after removing {E, G, P}:

        *   M -> J: J is connected to P (removed), D. No path to B.

    All paths from M to B are indeed broken by removing {E, G, P}.

5.  **Conclusion:** Since the minimum number of vertex-disjoint paths is 3, and I have found a cut set of size 3, this is the minimum number of nodes to remove.

Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are E and I. My goal is to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect E and I. I cannot remove E or I.

First, I will systematically identify all possible paths between E and I.
Let's list the direct neighbors of E: H, M, G.
Let's list the direct neighbors of I: H, J, C, P.

Any path from E to I must pass through one of E's neighbors, and eventually reach one of I's neighbors.

Let's analyze the main "channels" or "bottlenecks" for paths from E to I:

**Channel 1: Paths starting from E through H or G (which leads to H)**
*   **E - H - I**: This is a direct path.
*   **E - G - H - I**: Path through G to H.
*   **E - G - F - H - I**: Path through G and F to H.
*   **E - G - K - F - H - I**: Path through G, K, F to H.
*   **E - G - X - F - H - I**: Path through G, X, F to H.
    (Note: The nodes G, F, K, X form a clique. Any path from E through G to this cluster must eventually connect to H to reach I, as H is the only node in this cluster connected to I's side, and H is connected to G and F.)
*   Also, paths from I's side that eventually reach H and then E:
    *   **I - C - B - H - E**
    *   **I - C - N - B - H - E**
    *   **I - C - O - N - B - H - E**
    *   **I - P - O - N - B - H - E**
    *   **I - P - O - C - B - H - E**
    *   **I - P - O - C - N - B - H - E**
    All these paths in Channel 1 rely on node H to connect E and I. Therefore, removing node H would break all paths in this channel.

**Channel 2: Paths starting from E through M**
*   **E - M - J - I**: A path through M and J.
*   **E - M - J - P - I**: A path through M, J, and P.
*   **E - M - J - D - P - I**: A path through M, J, D, and P.
    All these paths in Channel 2 rely on node M (and subsequently J) to connect E and I.

**Summary of Channels:**
Every single path from E to I must pass through either node H (Channel 1) OR node M (Channel 2).

**Determining the Minimum Cut Set:**
1.  **Can we disconnect with 1 node?**
    *   If we remove H, all paths in Channel 1 are broken. However, paths in Channel 2 (e.g., E-M-J-I) would still exist. So, H alone is not enough.
    *   If we remove M, all paths in Channel 2 are broken. However, paths in Channel 1 (e.g., E-H-I) would still exist. So, M alone is not enough.
    *   If we remove J, all paths in Channel 2 are broken. However, paths in Channel 1 (e.g., E-H-I) would still exist. So, J alone is not enough.
    *   Removing any other single node (G, F, K, X, B, C, N, O, P, D, L, Y) would leave multiple paths intact through both Channel 1 and Channel 2.
    Therefore, a single node removal is not sufficient.

2.  **Can we disconnect with 2 nodes?**
    To disconnect E and I, we must break all paths in Channel 1 AND all paths in Channel 2.
    *   To break all paths in Channel 1, we must remove node H.
    *   To break all paths in Channel 2, we must remove a node that is common to all paths in that channel. Both M and J are such nodes. If we remove M, all paths E-M-J-...-I are broken. If we remove J, all paths E-M-J-...-I are also broken.

    Therefore, a cut set of size 2 can be formed by:
    *   Removing H (to break Channel 1) AND removing M (to break Channel 2). This gives the cut set [H, M].
    *   Removing H (to break Channel 1) AND removing J (to break Channel 2). This gives the cut set [H, J].

Both [H, M] and [H, J] are valid cut sets of size 2. Since we've established that 1 node is not enough, 2 is the minimum number of nodes.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [H, M]

Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 17
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are L and J. I need to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect L from J. I cannot remove L or J.

I will use Menger's theorem, which states that the minimum number of nodes to remove to disconnect two non-adjacent nodes (the node-connectivity) is equal to the maximum number of node-disjoint paths between them.

Let's identify node-disjoint paths between L and J (paths that share no intermediate nodes).

1.  **Path 1: L - M - J**
    This path uses the intermediate node M.

2.  **Path 2: L - E - C - I - J**
    Let's check if this path is node-disjoint from Path 1 (excluding L and J).
    Intermediate nodes in Path 1: {M}
    Intermediate nodes in Path 2: {E, C, I}
    These sets are disjoint. So, Path 2 is node-disjoint from Path 1.

3.  **Path 3: L - F - K - G - H - B - N - Y - O - P - J**
    Let's check if this path is node-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2.
    Intermediate nodes in Path 1: {M}
    Intermediate nodes in Path 2: {E, C, I}
    Intermediate nodes in Path 3: {F, K, G, H, B, N, Y, O, P}
    These three sets of intermediate nodes are all disjoint. Therefore, we have found 3 node-disjoint paths between L and J.

According to Menger's theorem, since there are 3 node-disjoint paths, the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect L and J is at least 3.

Now, I need to find a cut set of 3 nodes that achieves this disconnection.
Let's consider the nodes M, C, and H.

If I remove nodes M, C, and H:
*   **Path 1 (L - M - J):** This path is broken by the removal of M.
*   **Paths starting with L - E:**
    *   L - E - C - I - J: This path is broken by the removal of C.
    *   L - E - C - P - J: This path is broken by the removal of C.
    All paths from L through E must pass through C to reach J's neighbors (I or P). So, removing C disconnects L-E from J.
*   **Paths starting with L - F:**
    *   L - F - K - G - H - I - J: This path is broken by the removal of H.
    *   L - F - K - G - H - B - C - I - J: This path is broken by the removal of H or C.
    *   L - F - K - G - H - B - C - P - J: This path is broken by the removal of H or C.
    *   L - F - X - K - G - H - I - J: This path is broken by the removal of H.
    *   L - F - X - K - G - H - B - C - I - J: This path is broken by the removal of H or C.
    *   L - F - X - K - G - H - B - C - P - J: This path is broken by the removal of H or C.
    All paths from L through F eventually pass through H or C (via B). So, removing H and C disconnects L-F from J.

Since all identified paths are broken by the removal of M, C, and H, and we have established that at least 3 nodes must be removed, this set represents the minimum number of nodes.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [M, C, H]

The target nodes are E (black) and H (black). My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between E and H.

**Nodes directly connected to H:** B, G, I

1.  **Path through B directly:**
    *   E - B - H

2.  **Paths through F and G:**
    *   E - F - G - H
    *   E - F - X - G - H (alternative between F and G)
    *   E - F - K - G - H (alternative between F and G)

3.  **Paths through M/L and I:**
    *   E - M - J - P - I - H
    *   E - L - M - J - P - I - H

4.  **Paths through B and I (more complex):**
    *   E - B - N - C - I - H
    *   E - B - N - Y - O - P - I - H
    *   E - B - C - I - H
    *   E - B - C - O - P - I - H

To find the minimum number of edges to remove, I can look for edge-disjoint paths. If I can find 'k' edge-disjoint paths, then by Menger's theorem, the minimum cut size is at least 'k'.

Let's identify three edge-disjoint paths:
*   **Path 1:** E - B - H
    *   Edges: (E, B), (B, H)
*   **Path 2:** E - F - G - H
    *   Edges: (E, F), (F, G), (G, H)
*   **Path 3:** E - M - J - P - I - H
    *   Edges: (E, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, I), (I, H)

Upon inspection, these three paths do not share any common edges.
*   Path 1 uses edges (E,B) and (B,H).
*   Path 2 uses edges (E,F), (F,G), and (G,H).
*   Path 3 uses edges (E,M), (M,J), (J,P), (P,I), and (I,H).

Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between E and H, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect them is at least 3.

Now, I need to find a cut set of size 3. A straightforward approach is to remove all edges directly connected to one of the target nodes, provided that node has a degree equal to the minimum cut.
Node H has three direct connections: (B, H), (G, H), and (I, H).
If I remove these three edges, H becomes isolated from the rest of the graph, and thus from E.

Let's verify this cut set:
1.  **Remove (B, H):** This breaks the direct path E-B-H and any other paths that would use (B, H).
2.  **Remove (G, H):** This breaks all paths that lead to H via G, such as E-F-G-H, E-F-X-G-H, E-F-K-G-H.
3.  **Remove (I, H):** This breaks all paths that lead to H via I, such as E-M-J-P-I-H, E-L-M-J-P-I-H, E-B-N-C-I-H, E-B-N-Y-O-P-I-H, E-B-C-I-H, E-B-C-O-P-I-H.

After removing these three edges, H has no remaining connections to any other node in the graph. Therefore, no path can exist between E and H.
This cut set has a size of 3, which matches the lower bound established by the edge-disjoint paths.

Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are G and N. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between G and N.

1.  **Identify all direct connections from G:** G is connected to F, X, K, and H.
2.  **Identify all direct connections to N:** N is connected to B, C, Y, and O.
3.  **Categorize paths from G to N:** All paths from G to N must start by traversing one of G's outgoing edges: (G,F), (G,X), (G,K), or (G,H).

    *   **Group 1: Paths starting with (G,H).**
        *   G-H-B-N
        *   G-H-I-C-N
        *   G-H-I-J-P-O-N
        *   G-H-I-J-D-P-O-N
        All these paths share the initial edge (G,H). Removing (G,H) would disconnect G from H and thus from all subsequent nodes in these paths (B, I, C, J, P, D, O, and ultimately N).

    *   **Group 2: Paths starting with (G,F), (G,X), or (G,K).**
        Nodes F, X, and K are interconnected (F-X, F-K, X-K). So, G, F, X, K form a connected sub-component. Any path from G that goes through F, X, or K must eventually leave this sub-component to reach N.
        The only edges leading out of the {G,F,X,K} sub-component (excluding (G,H) which is in Group 1) are (F,E) and (F,M).
        *   If a path goes via (F,E): G-{F,X,K}-F-E-B-N.
        *   If a path goes via (F,M): G-{F,X,K}-F-M-L-E-B-N.
        Notice that both of these sub-paths eventually converge and pass through the edge (E,B) to reach N. Therefore, the edge (E,B) is a critical bottleneck for all paths in Group 2. Removing (E,B) would disconnect E from B, and thus prevent any path from G (via F, X, or K) from reaching N through B.

4.  **Formulate a cut set:** To disconnect G from N, we need to break all paths in Group 1 and all paths in Group 2.
    *   To break all paths in Group 1, we remove edge (G,H).
    *   To break all paths in Group 2, we remove edge (E,B).
    This gives us a cut set of [(G,H), (E,B)]. This set contains 2 edges.

5.  **Verify the cut set and minimality:**
    *   **Verification:**
        *   If (G,H) is removed, G cannot reach H, and therefore cannot reach B, I, C, J, D, P, O, or Y through H. This breaks all paths in Group 1.
        *   If (E,B) is removed, G cannot reach B via E. Since all paths from G through F, X, or K must pass through E then B to reach N, removing (E,B) breaks all paths in Group 2.
        *   Since all paths from G to N fall into either Group 1 or Group 2, removing these two edges successfully disconnects G from N.
    *   **Minimality:**
        *   Can we disconnect G and N by removing only 1 edge?
            *   If we remove (G,H), paths through F, X, K (e.g., G-F-E-B-N) still exist.
            *   If we remove (E,B), paths through H (e.g., G-H-B-N, G-H-I-C-N) still exist.
            *   If we remove any other single edge, it will only break a subset of paths, leaving others intact. For example, removing (N,B) would still leave paths like G-H-I-C-N and G-H-I-J-P-O-N.
        *   Therefore, 1 edge is not sufficient. The minimum number of edges is 2.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(G, H), (E, B)]

The neighbors of H are E, G, B, I.
The neighbors of N are B, C, O, Y.

Any path from H to N must pass through one of N's neighbors (B, C, O, Y) or an edge leading to them.

Let's analyze the main "channels" through which H can reach N:

1.  **Through Node B:**
    *   Path: H - B - N
    *   This path can be cut by removing edge (H, B) or (B, N).

2.  **Through Node C:**
    *   Path: H - I - C - N
    *   Path: H - B - C - N (If (H, B) is not cut)
    *   These paths can be cut by removing edges like (H, I), (I, C), (B, C), or (C, N).

3.  **Through Node O:**
    *   Path: H - I - P - O - N
    *   Path: H - I - C - O - N (If (I, C) is not cut)
    *   Path: H - B - C - O - N (If (H, B) and (B, C) are not cut)
    *   Paths through E: H - E - M - J - P - O - N
    *   Paths through G: H - G - F - E - M - J - P - O - N (and other variations through G, X, K)
    *   These paths can be cut by removing edges like (H, I), (I, P), (P, O), (H, E), (E, M), (M, J), (J, P), (H, G), (G, F), (F, E), or (O, N).

4.  **Through Node Y:**
    *   Path: H - ... - O - Y - N
    *   Any path to Y must first reach O. So, cutting paths to O will also cut paths to Y.

Let's try to find a minimal cut set.

**Attempt 1: Cut all edges directly connected to N.**
Removing (B, N), (C, N), (O, N), (Y, N) would disconnect N. This is 4 edges. We aim for a smaller number if possible.

**Attempt 2: Identify critical "bottleneck" edges.**
Consider the following three edges as candidates for a cut set:
*   **(H, B)**: This edge directly connects H to B, which is a neighbor of N.
*   **(I, C)**: This edge connects I (reachable from H) to C, which is a neighbor of N.
*   **(P, O)**: This edge connects P (reachable from H via I, E, or G) to O, which is a neighbor of N.

Let's verify if removing these three edges, **[(H, B), (I, C), (P, O)]**, disconnects H from N:

1.  **Paths through B to N:**
    *   The direct path H - B - N is broken by removing (H, B).
    *   Any path H - B - C - N or H - B - C - O - N or H - B - C - O - Y - N is also broken by removing (H, B).

2.  **Paths through C to N (not via B):**
    *   The path H - I - C - N is broken by removing (I, C).
    *   Any path H - I - C - O - N or H - I - C - O - Y - N is also broken by removing (I, C).

3.  **Paths through O to N (not via B or C):**
    *   The path H - I - P - O - N is broken by removing (P, O).
    *   All paths originating from H through E (e.g., H - E - M - J - P - O - N, H - E - L - M - J - P - O - N) are broken by removing (P, O).
    *   All paths originating from H through G (e.g., H - G - F - E - M - J - P - O - N, H - G - X - K - F - E - M - J - P - O - N) are broken by removing (P, O).

4.  **Paths through Y to N:**
    *   Any path to Y must pass through O (e.g., H - ... - O - Y - N). Since all paths to O are broken by (P, O), (I, C), or (H, B), all paths to Y are also broken.

Therefore, removing the edges (H, B), (I, C), and (P, O) successfully disconnects H from N. This is a cut set of size 3.

**Minimality Check:**
Could we disconnect H and N with only 2 edges?
Let's consider the three "channels" identified:
*   Channel 1: H-B-N
*   Channel 2: H-I-C-N
*   Channel 3: H-...-P-O-N (representing all paths through P to O and then N)

If we remove any two edges from our proposed cut set:
*   Removing (H, B) and (I, C): Path H - I - P - O - N (and all paths through E/G to P-O-N) would still exist.
*   Removing (H, B) and (P, O): Path H - I - C - N would still exist.
*   Removing (I, C) and (P, O): Path H - B - N would still exist.

Since removing any two edges leaves at least one path intact, a minimum of 3 edges is required.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(H, B), (I, C), (P, O)]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 3, 'cut_set': [[['F', 'E'], ['H', 'B'], ['H', 'I']], [['G', 'H'], ['H', 'B'], ['H', 'I']]]}

Step-by-step reasoning:
The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect the two black nodes, H and N.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their direct connections:**
    *   Target Node 1: H. Its direct neighbors are G, B, I. The edges are (H, G), (H, B), (H, I).
    *   Target Node 2: N. Its direct neighbors are B, C, Y, O. The edges are (N, B), (N, C), (N, Y), (N, O).

2.  **Consider isolating one of the target nodes:**
    *   To isolate H, we can remove all edges connected to it: (H, G), (H, B), (H, I). This would require removing 3 edges. After removing these, H would have no connections to any other node, thus disconnecting it from N.
    *   To isolate N, we can remove all edges connected to it: (N, B), (N, C), (N, Y), (N, O). This would require removing 4 edges.

    Since 3 is less than 4, a cut set of size 3 is a possible candidate. We need to determine if a smaller cut (size 1 or 2) is possible.

3.  **Attempt to find edge-disjoint paths (Menger's Theorem):**
    Menger's Theorem states that the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them. If we can find *k* edge-disjoint paths, then the minimum cut size is at least *k*.

    Let's identify edge-disjoint paths from H to N:

    *   **Path 1:** H - B - N
        Edges: {(H, B), (B, N)}

    *   **Path 2:** H - I - C - N
        Edges: {(H, I), (I, C), (C, N)}
        This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 (no common edges).

    *   **Path 3:** H - G - K - J - D - P - O - N
        Edges: {(H, G), (G, K), (K, J), (J, D), (D, P), (P, O), (O, N)}
        This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2 (no common edges).

    We have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between H and N. According to Menger's Theorem, this means that at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect H from N.

4.  **Conclusion:**
    Since we found a way to disconnect H and N by removing 3 edges (by isolating H), and we have proven that at least 3 edges must be removed (by finding 3 edge-disjoint paths), the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

    The cut set that achieves this minimum is the set of all edges directly connected to H.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(H, G), (H, B), (H, I)]

1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are H and B.

    *   **Path 2 (P2): H - E - B**

    *   **Path 4 (P4): H - G - F - E - L - M - J - P - C - B**

    Consider the following cut set: `C = {(H, B), (H, E), (H, I), (F, E)}`

    *   `(H, B)`: Breaks the direct connection.

    *   `(F, E)`: Breaks any path from H that goes through F and then E.

    *   Nodes directly connected to B are E, I, C, N (since (H,B) is removed).

    *   From G, we can reach X, K, F.
    *   From F, we can reach X, K, G.

*   Edges of P2: (F, E), (E, B), (B, N), (N, D)
*   Edges of P3: (F, G), (G, H), (H, I), (I, P), (P, D)

Comparing the edge sets, there are no common edges between any two of these paths. For example:
*   (F, M) is unique to P1. (F, E) is unique to P2. (F, G) is unique to P3.
*   (M, J) is unique to P1. (E, B) is unique to P2. (G, H) is unique to P3.
*   (J, D) is unique to P1. (B, N) is unique to P2. (H, I) is unique to P3.
*   (N, D) is unique to P2. (I, P) is unique to P3.
*   (P, D) is unique to P3.

Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect F from D is 3.

Next, I need to find a specific set of 3 edges whose removal achieves this disconnection. I will look for a cut set that separates the graph into two components, one containing F and the other containing D.

Let's define a partition of the nodes:
*   Set A (containing F): {F, M, L, E, G, X, K}
*   Set B (containing D): {D, J, P, Y, N, B, C, I, O, H}

Now, I identify all edges that connect a node in Set A to a node in Set B:
1.  **(M, J)**: M is in A, J is in B.
2.  **(E, B)**: E is in A, B is in B.
3.  **(G, H)**: G is in A, H is in B.

These three edges form a cut set. Let's verify that removing these edges disconnects F from D.
After removing [(M, J), (E, B), (G, H)]:
*   Any path from F that goes through M must use the edge (M, J) to reach the D-side of the graph. This path is cut.
*   Any path from F that goes through E must use the edge (E, B) to reach the D-side of the graph (nodes B, C, I, O, N, P, Y, J). This path is cut.
*   Any path from F that goes through G must use the edge (G, H) to reach the D-side of the graph (nodes H, B, C, I, O, N, P, Y, J). This path is cut.
*   Nodes L, X, K are only connected to other nodes within Set A (F, M, E, G). Therefore, any path through them would eventually lead to M, E, or G, which are then cut off from Set B.

Thus, removing these three edges completely disconnects F from D. Since we established that the minimum cut is 3, this set of edges is a minimal cut set.

        *   P6: H - I - P - O - N - B

2.  **Determine the minimum number of edge-disjoint paths:**
    To find the minimum cut, it's helpful to identify edge-disjoint paths. If there are `k` edge-disjoint paths, then at least `k` edges must be removed to disconnect the two nodes.
    Let's try to find edge-disjoint paths:
    *   Path 1: H - B (edges: `(H, B)`)
    *   Path 2: H - G - F - E - B (edges: `(H, G)`, `(G, F)`, `(F, E)`, `(E, B)`)

    Since we have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between H and B, by Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect H and B is at least 3.

3.  **Find a cut set of size 3:**

    By removing these three edges, H becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph, and therefore from B. This is a valid cut set of size 3.

4.  **Conclusion:**
    Since the minimum number of edges required is at least 3 (from Menger's Theorem) and we have found a cut set of size 3, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

        *   G-F-M-D-P-O-N (This path does not use J)
        All these paths from G to O that pass through P will be severed by removing node **P**.

7.  **Formulate a Candidate Cut Set:** Based on the analysis above, removing nodes {B, I, P} should disconnect G from N.
    *   Removing B cuts all paths to N via B.
    *   Removing I cuts all paths to N via C (and also some paths to O via C).
    *   Removing P cuts all paths to N via O that pass through P. Since I is also removed, G cannot reach O via C either. Therefore, all paths to O (and Y) are cut.

8.  **Verify the Cut Set {B, I, P}:**
    *   **Paths to B:** All paths to B (G-F-E-B, G-F-M-L-E-B, G-F-M-L-B, G-H-B) are cut by removing B.
    *   **Paths to C:** All paths to C (e.g., G-H-I-C, G-F-K-J-I-C) must pass through I. Removing I cuts these paths.
    *   **Paths to O (and Y):**
        *   Paths via P (e.g., G-F-M-D-P-O, G-F-K-J-P-O) are cut by removing P.
        *   Paths via C (e.g., G-H-I-C-O) are cut by removing I (as G cannot reach C).
        *   Since all paths to P and C are cut, all paths to O are cut. Consequently, paths to Y are also cut.
    Therefore, removing {B, I, P} successfully disconnects G from N. This is a cut set of size 3.

9.  **Check for a Smaller Cut Set (Size 1 or 2):**
    *   **Size 1:** No single node can disconnect G from N, as there are multiple independent "channels" (e.g., through B, through C, through O). For example, removing B leaves paths through C and O.
    *   **Size 2:** Consider three relatively node-disjoint paths from G to N:
        *   Path 1: G-H-B-N (requires B to be cut)
        *   Path 2: G-H-I-C-N (requires I to be cut)
        *   Path 3: G-F-M-D-P-O-N (requires P to be cut)
        These three paths are node-disjoint except for G. To cut all three, at least one node from {B, I, P} must be removed for each path. Since these are distinct nodes, a minimum of 3 nodes are required. For example:
        *   Removing {B, I} leaves Path 3 intact.
        *   Removing {B, P} leaves Path 2 intact.
        *   Removing {I, P} leaves Path 1 intact.
    Therefore, a cut set of size 2 is not possible.

10. **Conclusion:** The minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

The final answer is \boxed{Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [B, I, P]}

    Edges involved: (B, H), (H, G), (G, F), (F, M)
3.  **Path 3 (via F, alternative):** B - H - G - X - F - M
    Edges involved: (B, H), (H, G), (G, X), (X, F), (F, M)
4.  **Path 4 (via L):** B - N - L - M
    Edges involved: (B, N), (N, L), (L, M)

I need to find a set of edges whose removal breaks all these paths. To find the minimum number of edges, I can look for edge-disjoint paths. If I can find 'k' edge-disjoint paths, then at least 'k' edges must be removed.

Let's identify edge-disjoint paths:
-   **Path A:** B - E - M (Edges: (B, E), (E, M))
-   **Path B:** B - N - L - M (Edges: (B, N), (N, L), (L, M))
-   **Path C:** B - H - G - F - M (Edges: (B, H), (H, G), (G, F), (F, M))

These three paths (Path A, Path B, Path C) are completely edge-disjoint. This means they do not share any common edges.
-   Path A uses edges (B, E) and (E, M).
-   Path B uses edges (B, N), (N, L), and (L, M).
-   Path C uses edges (B, H), (H, G), (G, F), and (F, M).

Since there are 3 edge-disjoint paths between B and M, according to Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect B and M is at least 3.

Now, I need to find a cut set of 3 edges that achieves this disconnection. A straightforward approach is to remove all edges directly connected to one of the target nodes. In this case, M has three incoming/outgoing edges: (E, M), (F, M), and (L, M).

-   Removing (F, M) breaks Path 2 (B-H-G-F-M) and Path 3 (B-H-G-X-F-M).
-   Removing (L, M) breaks Path 4 (B-N-L-M).

All identified paths between B and M are broken by removing these three edges. Since we've established that at least 3 edges are required, and we found a set of 3 edges that works, this is the minimum.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(E, M), (F, M), (L, M)]

    Node E is connected to four other nodes: B, F, M, and L.
    The edges connected to E are: (E, B), (E, F), (E, M), and (E, L).

2.  **Analyze the role of each edge connected to E in forming paths from H:**
    *   **(E, B):** This edge is part of paths such as H-B-E, H-I-C-B-E, H-I-C-N-B-E, H-I-P-O-C-B-E.
    *   **(E, F):** This edge is part of paths such as H-G-F-E, H-G-X-F-E, H-G-K-F-E.
    *   **(E, M):** This edge is part of paths such as H-I-J-M-E, H-I-P-J-M-E.
    *   **(E, L):** Node L is only connected to M and E. Any path from H to L must first pass through M (e.g., H-...-J-M-L-E). If the edge (E, M) is removed, then M is disconnected from E, and consequently, L (which depends on M for connection to the rest of the graph) would also be disconnected from the part of the graph containing H. Therefore, removing (E, L) is redundant if (E, M) is already removed, or if (E, M) is not removed, then (E, L) only cuts paths that specifically go through L, while paths directly through M (H-...-M-E) would remain. To ensure all paths through M (and L) are cut, removing (E, M) is the more comprehensive choice.

3.  **Identify the critical "entry points" to E:**
    Based on the analysis, any path from H to E must eventually pass through one of the nodes B, F, or M, and then use the corresponding edge to E: (B,E), (F,E), or (M,E). These three edges represent distinct "bottlenecks" for different groups of paths leading to E.

4.  **Test for a cut of size 1:**
    *   If we remove (B,E), paths through F and M to E still exist (e.g., H-G-F-E, H-I-J-M-E).
    *   If we remove (F,E), paths through B and M to E still exist (e.g., H-B-E, H-I-J-M-E).
    *   If we remove (M,E), paths through B and F to E still exist (e.g., H-B-E, H-G-F-E).
    Therefore, removing a single edge is not sufficient to disconnect H and E.

5.  **Test for a cut of size 2:**
    *   If we remove any two of the three critical edges [(B,E), (F,E), (M,E)], the third one will remain, allowing a path to exist. For example, removing (B,E) and (F,E) would still leave paths like H-I-J-M-E active via (M,E).
    *   This demonstrates that at least three edges are required to cut all paths that enter E through B, F, or M.

6.  **Test for a cut of size 3:**
    *   If we remove the three edges [(B,E), (F,E), (M,E)], node E becomes disconnected from B, F, and M.
    *   As established in step 2, since L is only connected to M and E, and (E,M) is now removed, L is also effectively disconnected from the part of the graph containing H. Thus, no path can reach E via L either.
    *   Therefore, removing the set of edges [(B,E), (F,E), (M,E)] completely disconnects E from H.

7.  **Conclusion:** The minimum number of edges that must be removed is 3.

An alternative minimal cut set of size 3 would be to remove all edges directly connected to H: [(H,G), (H,I), (H,B)]. This would isolate H from the rest of the graph, including E. Both are valid minimal solutions.

1.  **Identify direct neighbors of N and G:**
    *   N is connected to Y, O, B.
    *   G is connected to X, K, H.

2.  **Analyze paths to G's neighbors:**
    *   To reach G, any path must pass through H, K, or X.
    *   **Paths to K or X:**
        *   Nodes K and X are directly connected to F.
        *   Node F is directly connected to E.
        *   Node E is connected to B and L.
        *   Node L is connected to M.
        *   Node M is connected to O and J.
        *   Node B is connected to N, E, C.
        *   Node O is connected to N, B, C, M, P, Y.
        *   Node Y is connected to N, P.
        *   Tracing back, any path to K or X must eventually pass through F. The only edge connecting to F from the "N-side" of the graph is (E, F). Therefore, the edge (E, F) is a critical bottleneck for all paths leading to K or X (and subsequently to G). If (E, F) is removed, no path can reach K or X from N.
    *   **Paths to H:**
        *   Node H is connected to C, I, G.
        *   Node I is connected to C, J, H.
        *   Node C is connected to N, O, B, I, H.
        *   Node J is connected to D, P, M, I.
        *   Node D is connected to J, P.
        *   Node P is connected to O, Y, D, J.
        *   Node M is connected to O, L, J.

3.  **Search for edge-disjoint paths:**
    I will attempt to find edge-disjoint paths between N and G. If I can find `k` edge-disjoint paths, then the minimum cut size is at least `k`.

    *   **Path 1 (P1):** Let's try a path that goes through K.
        N - B - E - F - K - G
        Edges in P1: `(N, B), (B, E), (E, F), (F, K), (K, G)`

    *   **Path 2 (P2):** Let's try a path that goes through H and is edge-disjoint from P1.
        N - O - C - H - G
        Edges in P2: `(N, O), (O, C), (C, H), (H, G)`
        Checking for common edges with P1:
        `(N, B)` vs `(N, O)` - Disjoint
        `(B, E)` vs `(O, C)` - Disjoint
        `(E, F)` vs `(C, H)` - Disjoint
        `(F, K)` vs `(H, G)` - Disjoint
        `(K, G)` vs `(H, G)` - Disjoint
        P1 and P2 are indeed edge-disjoint. This means the minimum number of edges to remove is at least 2.

    *   **Path 3 (P3):** Now, I need to find a third path that is edge-disjoint from both P1 and P2.
        Edges used by P1: `(N, B), (B, E), (E, F), (F, K), (K, G)`
        Edges used by P2: `(N, O), (O, C), (C, H), (H, G)`

        *   Consider paths starting with `N - Y`:
            N - Y - P - J - I - H - G
            This path uses `(H, G)`, which is an edge in P2. Therefore, this path is not edge-disjoint from P2.

        *   Consider paths going through X (which is connected to G):
            As established in step 2, all paths to K or X must use the edge `(E, F)`. Since `(E, F)` is already part of P1, no other edge-disjoint path can use K or X to reach G.

        Since all paths to K or X must use `(E, F)` (which is in P1), and the only remaining way to reach G is through H, any third edge-disjoint path would have to go through H. However, we already found P2 going through H. Any other path to H would likely share edges with P2 (like `(H, G)`) or P1 (like `(N,B)` if we try `N-B-C-I-H-G`).

        It appears that I cannot find a third path that is completely edge-disjoint from both P1 and P2. This suggests that the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths is 2. By Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove is 2.

4.  **Identify a minimal cut set of size 2:**
    Based on the analysis, we need to find two edges whose removal disconnects N and G.
    *   From step 2, we know that removing `(E, F)` will cut all paths to K and X.
    *   To cut all remaining paths (which must go through H), we need to cut an edge that is common to all paths to H. The edge `(H, G)` is the final connection to G from H. If `(H, G)` is removed, no path can reach G via H.

    Let's propose the cut set: `[(E, F), (H, G)]`.

5.  **Verify the proposed cut set:**
    *   **Removal of (E, F):** This edge disconnects E from F. As established, all paths from N to K or X (and thus to G) must pass through E then F. By removing `(E, F)`, all paths to K and X are severed.
        *   Example paths cut: N-B-E-F-K-G, N-B-E-F-X-G, N-O-M-L-E-F-K-G, N-O-M-L-E-F-X-G, etc.
    *   **Removal of (H, G):** This edge disconnects H from G. All paths from N to H (and thus to G) must pass through H and then use the edge `(H, G)`. By removing `(H, G)`, all paths to H are severed from G.
        *   Example paths cut: N-B-C-H-G, N-O-C-H-G, N-Y-P-J-I-H-G, etc.

    Since G is only connected to K, X, and H, and all paths to these nodes are cut by removing `(E, F)` and `(H, G)`, N and G are completely disconnected. This cut set has 2 edges.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(E, F), (H, G)]

From G, all paths lead to H (G-H).
From H, all paths lead to B (H-B).
So, all paths in this bundle follow the sequence: F -> {X, K} -> G -> H -> B -> ... -> J.

To disconnect all paths in Bundle 2, I need to remove at least one edge that is common to all of them. The edges (G, H) or (H, B) are critical bottlenecks for all paths in this bundle. For example, removing the edge (G, H) would break all paths in Bundle 2.

Now, I combine these observations to find a minimal cut set.
To disconnect F from J, I must break paths from both Bundle 1 and Bundle 2.

If I choose to break Bundle 1 by removing (F, E), and Bundle 2 by removing (G, H), the cut set would be **[(F, E), (G, H)]**.
Let's verify this:
1. Removing (F, E) disconnects F from E, thus breaking paths F-E-M-J and F-E-L-M-J.
2. Removing (G, H) disconnects G from H, thus breaking all paths that go through F-{X,K}-G-H-B-...-J.

Since these two edges effectively break all identified paths between F and J, and no single edge can achieve this (as there are at least two distinct bundles of paths), the minimum number of edges to remove is 2.

Other valid cut sets of size 2 could be:
- [(F, E), (H, B)]
- [(M, J), (G, H)]
- [(M, J), (H, B)]

I will choose one for the final answer.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(F, E), (G, H)]
--- Ground Truth ---

1.  **Channel via M/L:**
    *   Path 1: J - M - E
    *   Path 2: J - M - L - E
    To cut all paths through M/L, we can either remove the edge (J, M) (1 edge), or remove both (M, E) and (L, E) (2 edges). The most efficient way to cut this channel is to remove **(J, M)**.

2.  **Channel via B:**
    *   Many paths from J lead to B, and then B connects to E via the edge (B, E). Examples:
        *   J - I - H - B - E
        *   J - I - C - B - E
        *   J - P - C - B - E
        *   J - D - P - O - N - B - E
    To cut all paths that go through B to reach E, we must remove the edge **(B, E)**.

        *   J - I - H - G - K - F - E
        *   J - P - I - H - G - K - F - E
        *   J - D - P - I - H - G - K - F - E
    To cut all paths that go through F to reach E, we must remove the edge **(F, E)**.

*   Any path starting from J must go through M, D, P, or I.
*   Any path ending at E must come from M, L, F, or B.

By selecting one edge from each of these critical channels, we can disconnect J from E.
The chosen edges are: **(J, M)**, **(B, E)**, and **(F, E)**.

Let's verify that removing these three edges disconnects J and E:
*   Removing (J, M) breaks all paths that start by connecting J to M (e.g., J-M-E, J-M-L-E).

*   Removing (F, E) breaks all paths that reach E via node F (e.g., J-I-H-G-K-F-E).

Since these three edges cover all the distinct "entry points" to E from J's side (M, B, F), no path can exist between J and E after their removal.

*   **(J, M)** and **(F, E)**: The path J - I - H - B - E would still exist.
*   **(B, E)** and **(F, E)**: The path J - M - E would still exist.

    *   Paths through G: F -> G -> ... -> J (Note: X and K are connected to G, so paths through X or K will eventually lead to G if they are to reach J via this branch).

    I will systematically check if removing any single node disconnects F and J.
    *   Removing E: F can still reach J via F -> G -> H -> I -> J (and other paths through G).
    *   Removing G: F can still reach J via F -> E -> M -> J (and other paths through E).

5.  **Explore for a two-node cut:**

    Let's consider the two main "entry points" from F to the rest of the graph: E and G.
    *   **Consider removing E and H:**
        *   If **E** is removed: All paths starting with F -> E -> ... -> J are broken.
            The remaining paths from F to J must go through F's other neighbors: X, K, or G. All these paths converge at G (e.g., F-X-G, F-K-G, F-G).
            So, all remaining paths from F to J must follow the pattern: F -> {X, K, G} -> G -> H -> ... -> J.
        *   If **H** is also removed: All paths starting with F -> G -> H -> ... -> J are broken.

            F is connected to {X, K, G}.
            G is connected to {F, X, K}. (H is removed, so G has no connection to the "middle" part of the graph towards J).
            X is connected to {F, K, G}.
            K is connected to {F, X, G}.
            Therefore, the set of nodes {F, X, K, G} is completely isolated from the rest of the graph that leads to J.
            J is connected to {M, I, P, D}.
            Since there are no paths from {F, X, K, G} to any node connected to J (because E and H are removed), F and J are completely disconnected.

6.  **Verify minimality:**
    I have confirmed that a single node removal is not sufficient. I have found a set of two nodes ([E, H]) that successfully disconnects F and J. Therefore, this is the minimum number of nodes.

    *   Node J is connected to: M, D, P, I.

Node D is connected to J but is not reachable from E (as D is only connected to J). Therefore, any path from E to J must eventually pass through M, P, or I before reaching J.

3.  **Formulate a hypothesis for a minimal cut set:**
    A straightforward approach to disconnect J from E is to remove all edges connecting J to nodes that are reachable from E. These edges are (J, M), (J, P), and (J, I).

4.  **Verify the proposed cut set:**
    Let's examine if removing the edges [(J, M), (J, P), (J, I)] successfully disconnects E from J.
    *   **Removing (J, M):** This breaks all paths that use the direct connection between M and J. Examples: E-M-J, E-L-M-J.
    *   **Removing (J, P):** This breaks all paths that use the direct connection between P and J. Examples: E-P-J, E-B-C-O-P-J, E-B-H-G-F-P-J, etc.
    *   **Removing (J, I):** This breaks all paths that use the direct connection between I and J. Examples: E-B-C-I-J, E-B-H-I-J.

    After removing these three edges, node J is only connected to node D. Node D is not connected to E, nor is it connected to any other node that could form a path back to E. Therefore, E and J are completely disconnected. This cut set has a size of 3.

5.  **Determine if this cut set is minimal (i.e., if a smaller cut exists):**
    To prove that 3 is the minimum number of edges, I need to find at least 3 edge-disjoint paths between E and J. If such paths exist, then by Menger's Theorem, at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect E and J.

    Let's identify three edge-disjoint paths:
    *   **Path 1:** E - M - J (Edges: (E, M), (M, J))
    *   **Path 2:** E - P - J (Edges: (E, P), (P, J))
    *   **Path 3:** E - B - H - I - J (Edges: (E, B), (B, H), (H, I), (I, J))

    *   Edges in Path 2: {(E, P), (P, J)}
    *   Edges in Path 3: {(E, B), (B, H), (H, I), (I, J)}

    None of the edges in Path 1 are present in Path 2 or Path 3.
    None of the edges in Path 2 are present in Path 1 or Path 3.
    None of the edges in Path 3 are present in Path 1 or Path 2.
    Thus, these three paths are indeed edge-disjoint.

    Since there are at least 3 edge-disjoint paths between E and J, the minimum number of edges required to disconnect them is at least 3. As we found a cut set of size 3, this is the minimum.

    *   Neighbors of H: G, B, I

3.  **Search for edge-disjoint paths:**
    According to Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them. Let's try to find edge-disjoint paths from E to H:
    *   **Path 1:** E - B - H
        Edges: {(E,B), (B,H)}
    *   **Path 2:** E - I - H
        Edges: {(E,I), (I,H)}
    *   **Path 3:** E - F - G - H
        Edges: {(E,F), (F,G), (G,H)}

    Let's verify if these three paths are edge-disjoint:
    *   The first edges from E are (E,B), (E,I), and (E,F), which are all distinct.
    *   The last edges to H are (B,H), (I,H), and (G,H), which are all distinct.
    *   The intermediate edges (F,G) in Path 3 do not overlap with any edges in Path 1 or Path 2.
    Therefore, these three paths are indeed edge-disjoint.

4.  **Determine the minimum cut size:**
    Since we have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between E and H, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect them is at least 3.

5.  **Identify a minimal cut set:**
    As identified in step 2, removing the three edges directly connected to H: [(G,H), (B,H), (I,H)] will completely isolate H. This set has a size of 3, which matches our minimum determined in step 4.

6.  **Verify the chosen cut set:**
    *   Removing (G,H) breaks all paths that end via G.
    *   Removing (B,H) breaks all paths that end via B.
    *   Removing (I,H) breaks all paths that end via I.
    Since H is only connected to G, B, and I, removing these three edges ensures that no path can reach H from E (or any other node).

Final Answer:

- Neighbors of N: Y, O, B
- Neighbors of H: B, G, I

Any path from N to H must start from N and eventually reach H. This means any path must pass through one of N's neighbors (Y, O, or B) and eventually connect to H through one of H's neighbors (B, G, or I).

Let's list the distinct paths from N to H, categorizing them by the neighbor of H they use for the final connection:

**Paths connecting to H via B:**
1.  N - B - H (Direct path)
2.  N - O - C - B - H
3.  N - O - P - J - M - E - B - H
4.  N - Y - O - C - B - H
5.  N - Y - O - P - J - M - E - B - H

**Paths connecting to H via I:**
1.  N - O - C - I - H
2.  N - O - P - I - H
3.  N - Y - O - C - I - H
4.  N - Y - O - P - I - H

**Paths connecting to H via G:**
1.  N - O - P - J - M - E - F - X - K - G - H
2.  N - Y - O - P - J - M - E - F - X - K - G - H

From this analysis, it is clear that all paths from N to H must use one of the following three edges to make the final connection to H:
-   (B, H)
-   (I, H)
-   (G, H)

If we remove all three of these edges, H will be completely isolated from the rest of the graph, and therefore from N. This set of edges, [(B, H), (I, H), (G, H)], constitutes a cut set of size 3.

Now, I need to verify if this is the *minimum* number of edges. I will check if removing any two of these three edges would still leave a path between N and H:

1.  **Remove (B, H) and (I, H):**
    Paths that connect to H via G would still exist. For example, the path N - O - P - J - M - E - F - X - K - G - H would still be intact. Thus, N and H would not be disconnected.

2.  **Remove (B, H) and (G, H):**
    Paths that connect to H via I would still exist. For example, the path N - O - C - I - H would still be intact. Thus, N and H would not be disconnected.

3.  **Remove (I, H) and (G, H):**
    Paths that connect to H via B would still exist. For example, the path N - B - H would still be intact. Thus, N and H would not be disconnected.

Since removing any two of these three edges leaves at least one path between N and H, all three edges are necessary to completely disconnect N from H. Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

An alternative minimal cut set of size 3 would be to remove all edges directly connected to N: [(N, B), (N, O), (N, Y)]. This would isolate N from the rest of the graph.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(B, H), (I, H), (G, H)]

1.  **Identify direct connections of target nodes:**

    *   Node B is connected to E, H, C, N.

2.  **Determine the lower bound for the cut set size:**
    *   If we remove all edges connected to M, we remove 3 edges: (M,E), (M,L), (M,K). This disconnects M.
    *   If we remove all edges connected to B, we remove 4 edges: (B,E), (B,H), (B,C), (B,N). This disconnects B.
    *   Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is at most 3.
    *   Can it be 1? No, because M has degree 3 and B has degree 4, and there is no single edge that acts as a bridge between M and B (e.g., removing (E,B) still leaves paths via H, and removing (M,E) still leaves paths via L and K). So, the minimum is at least 2.

3.  **Systematically explore paths and identify critical edges:**
    Let's consider all possible paths from M to B. Any path from M to B must start from one of M's neighbors (E, L, K) and eventually reach one of B's neighbors (E, H, C, N).

    Let's analyze the structure of the graph to find "bottleneck" edges.
    Consider the nodes E and H. It appears that all paths from M to B must pass through either E or H. Let's verify this:

    *   **Paths starting M -> E:**
        *   M-E-B: This path directly uses the edge (E,B).
        *   M-E-F-G-H-B: This path uses the edge (G,H) to reach H, and then H to B (either directly H-B or via H-I-C-B, etc.).
        *   M-E-F-X-G-H-B: This path also uses (G,H). (Note: M-E-F-X-K-M is a cycle, not towards B).

    *   **Paths starting M -> L:**
        *   M-L-E-B: This path uses the edge (E,B).
        *   M-L-E-F-G-H-B: This path uses the edge (G,H).

    *   **Paths starting M -> K:**
        *   M-K-X-F-E-B: This path uses the edge (E,B).
        *   M-K-X-G-H-B: This path uses the edge (G,H).

    *   **Paths from H to B via I, C, P, O, N:**
        Any path from M that reaches H (e.g., M-K-X-G-H or M-E-F-G-H) can then proceed to B via:
        *   H-B (direct)
        *   H-I-C-B
        *   H-I-P-C-B
        *   H-I-P-O-N-B
        *   H-I-C-N-B
        All these paths from M to H to B must have passed through (G,H) to reach H from the M-side of the graph.

    Let's formalize the argument:
    Assume there exists a path from M to B.
    *   If this path does NOT use the edge (G,H):
        Then it must reach B without passing through G and then H.
        The only way for M to reach B without using (G,H) is to go through E.
        Specifically, paths like M-E-B, M-L-E-B, M-K-X-F-E-B. All these paths use the edge (E,B) to connect to B.
        Therefore, if a path does not use (G,H), it must use (E,B).
    *   If this path does NOT use the edge (E,B):

        The only way for M to reach B without using (E,B) is to go through H.
        Specifically, paths like M-E-F-G-H-B, M-L-E-F-G-H-B, M-K-X-G-H-B, and any paths from H to B via I, C, P, O, N. All these paths must use the edge (G,H) to connect to H from the M-side of the graph.
        Therefore, if a path does not use (E,B), it must use (G,H).

    Since every path from M to B must either use (E,B) or (G,H), removing both of these edges will guarantee that no path exists between M and B.

4.  **Formulate the cut set and verify minimality:**
    The cut set is [(E,B), (G,H)].
    *   Removing (E,B) cuts all paths that directly connect E to B.

Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are G and I. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect G from I.

1.  **Identify all possible paths between G and I:**
    First, I observe the direct neighbors of G: K, X, F, E, H.
    And the direct neighbors of I: H, E, J, P, C.

    I notice that nodes E and H are common neighbors to both G's neighborhood and I's neighborhood. This suggests they are critical for many paths.

    Let's list some distinct paths from G to I:
    *   Path 1: G - E - I
    *   Path 2: G - H - I
    *   Path 3: G - F - E - M - J - I (This path uses F, then E, then M, J, and finally I)

2.  **Determine the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths:**
    To find the minimum edge cut, I can use Menger's theorem, which states that the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them.

    Let's check if the three paths identified above are edge-disjoint:
    *   Edges in Path 1 (P1): {(G, E), (E, I)}
    *   Edges in Path 2 (P2): {(G, H), (H, I)}
    *   Edges in Path 3 (P3): {(G, F), (F, E), (E, M), (M, J), (J, I)}

    Let's compare the edge sets:
    *   P1 and P2: No common edges. (G,E) != (G,H), (E,I) != (H,I).
    *   P1 and P3: No common edges. (G,E) is not in P3. (E,I) is not in P3.
    *   P2 and P3: No common edges. (G,H) is not in P3. (H,I) is not in P3.

    Since I have found three edge-disjoint paths, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect G and I is at least 3.

3.  **Find a cut set of 3 edges and verify its effectiveness:**
    Now that I know the minimum is at least 3, I need to find a set of 3 edges whose removal disconnects G from I.

    Consider the "entry points" from G's immediate neighborhood to the rest of the graph leading to I. These are the edges (G, E), (G, H), and (G, F). However, removing these three would still leave paths like G-K-F-E-I.

    Let's consider the nodes E and H. All paths from G to I must eventually pass through either E or H.
    *   If a path from G goes through F, K, or X, it must then connect to E to proceed towards I (e.g., G-F-E-I).
    *   If a path from G goes through E, it can go directly to I (G-E-I) or through H (G-E-H-I) or through M, J, P, C to I.
    *   If a path from G goes through H, it can go directly to I (G-H-I) or through E (G-H-E-I) or through B, C, N, O, P, J to I.

    Therefore, to disconnect G from I, we must ensure G cannot reach E and G cannot reach H.

    Let's propose the following cut set: **[(E, G), (G, H), (E, F)]**.

    Let's verify this cut set:
    *   **Remove (E, G):** This breaks all direct paths from G to E.
    *   **Remove (G, H):** This breaks all direct paths from G to H.
    *   **Remove (E, F):** This breaks all paths from F to E.

    After removing these three edges:
    *   G is now connected only to K and X.
    *   K is connected to G, F, X.
    *   X is connected to G, K, F.
    *   F is connected to K, X. (Crucially, F is no longer connected to E, and G is no longer connected to F via (G,F) if that was an edge, but it's not. G is connected to F, but F is no longer connected to E).

    Let's trace paths from G to I after the removal:
    *   Can G reach E?
        *   Directly: No, (E, G) is removed.
        *   Via F: G is connected to F (via (G,F), (G,K,F), (G,X,F)). But F is no longer connected to E because (E, F) is removed. So, G cannot reach E via F.
        *   Via H: G cannot reach H directly because (G, H) is removed. G cannot reach H via E because G cannot reach E.
    *   Can G reach H?
        *   Directly: No, (G, H) is removed.
        *   Via E: G cannot reach E (as shown above), so it cannot reach H via E.

    Since G cannot reach E and G cannot reach H, and all paths from G to I must pass through E or H, G is completely disconnected from I.

    This cut set of 3 edges successfully disconnects G from I, and we've established that the minimum is at least 3. Therefore, this is the minimum cut.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(E, G), (G, H), (E, F)]

1.  **Identify direct connections to the target nodes:**
    *   Node F is connected to G, K, X, P.
    *   Node D is connected to J, P.

2.  **Analyze paths and potential bottlenecks:**
    Any path from F to D must eventually pass through one of the nodes directly connected to D, which are P or J. Therefore, all paths to D must use either the edge (P, D) or the edge (J, D) as their final segment.

3.  **Consider a cut set by isolating one target node:**
    A straightforward way to disconnect D from F (and the rest of the graph) is to remove all edges directly connected to D. These edges are (P, D) and (J, D).
    *   If we remove (P, D), any path that used this edge is broken.
    *   If we remove (J, D), any path that used this edge is broken.
    *   By removing both (P, D) and (J, D), node D becomes completely isolated, meaning no path can reach it from F or any other node in the graph.
    This cut set, [(P, D), (J, D)], has a size of 2.

4.  **Determine if a smaller cut is possible (e.g., 1 edge):**
    To prove that 2 is the minimum, I need to show that removing only one edge is not sufficient to disconnect F and D. Let's identify at least two edge-disjoint paths between F and D.
    *   Path 1: F - P - D (uses edges (F, P) and (P, D))
    *   Path 2: F - G - H - I - J - D (uses edges (F, G), (G, H), (H, I), (I, J), (J, D))
    These two paths are edge-disjoint (they do not share any common edges). Therefore, removing any single edge can only break at most one of these paths, leaving the other intact. This means a single edge removal is insufficient to disconnect F and D.

5.  **Conclusion:**
    Since 1 edge is not enough, and we found a cut set of 2 edges that successfully disconnects F and D, the minimum number of edges to remove is 2.

1.  **I - P - E**: This is a direct path through node P.
2.  **I - J - M - E**: This path goes through J and M.
3.  **I - C - B - E**: This path goes through C and B.
4.  **I - H - C - B - E**: This path goes through H, then C, then B. Note that this path shares the C-B-E segment with path 3.

To disconnect I and E, all these paths must be broken. I can identify three distinct "bottleneck" points or sets of nodes that must be traversed by these paths:

*   **Bottleneck 1 (for path 1):** Node P. Any path using I-P-E must pass through P.
*   **Bottleneck 2 (for path 2):** Nodes J or M. Any path using I-J-M-E must pass through J and M. Removing either J or M will break this path.
*   **Bottleneck 3 (for paths 3 and 4):** Nodes C or B. Any path using I-C-B-E or I-H-C-B-E must pass through C and B. Removing either C or B will break these paths.

Let's test if a cut set of size 1 or 2 is sufficient:

*   **Size 1:**
    *   Removing P: Paths I-J-M-E, I-C-B-E, I-H-C-B-E still exist. (Not enough)
    *   Removing J (or M): Paths I-P-E, I-C-B-E, I-H-C-B-E still exist. (Not enough)
    *   Removing C (or B): Paths I-P-E, I-J-M-E still exist. (Not enough)
    Therefore, a single node removal is not sufficient.

*   **Size 2:**
    *   Removing P and J: Paths I-C-B-E, I-H-C-B-E still exist. (Not enough)
    *   Removing P and M: Paths I-C-B-E, I-H-C-B-E still exist. (Not enough)
    *   Removing P and C: Path I-J-M-E still exists. (Not enough)
    *   Removing P and B: Path I-J-M-E still exists. (Not enough)
    *   Removing J and M: Path I-P-E, I-C-B-E, I-H-C-B-E still exist. (Not enough)
    *   Removing J and C: Path I-P-E still exists. (Not enough)
    *   Removing J and B: Path I-P-E still exists. (Not enough)
    *   Removing M and C: Path I-P-E still exists. (Not enough)
    *   Removing M and B: Path I-P-E still exists. (Not enough)
    *   Removing C and B: Paths I-P-E, I-J-M-E still exist. (Not enough)
    Therefore, a two-node removal is not sufficient.

Since a cut set of size 1 or 2 is not sufficient, I must consider a cut set of size 3.
Based on the identified bottlenecks, to break all paths, I need to:
1.  Remove P (to break I-P-E).
2.  Remove either J or M (to break I-J-M-E).
3.  Remove either C or B (to break I-C-B-E and I-H-C-B-E).

This requires selecting one node from each of these three independent bottleneck groups. Thus, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

*   Removing J breaks the path I-J-M-E.
*   Removing C breaks the paths I-C-B-E and I-H-C-B-E.

    Similarly, any path from B to M must end by traversing an edge from one of B's neighbors (E, H, N, C) to B.

4.  **Consider removing a single node:**
    *   If we remove **E**: The direct path M-E-B is broken. However, paths like M-F-G-H-B or M-F-K-J-I-C-B would still exist. Thus, removing E alone is not sufficient.
    *   If we remove **F**: All paths starting M-F-... are broken. However, the path M-E-B would still exist. Thus, removing F alone is not sufficient.
    *   If we remove **L**: L is connected to M, F, and E. Paths like M-L-F-... or M-L-E-... would be broken, but paths like M-F-... and M-E-... would still exist. Thus, removing L alone is not sufficient.
    *   If we remove any other single node (H, N, C, G, K, X, J, I, P, O, D, Y), there will always be at least one path remaining through E or F. For example, removing H leaves M-E-B, M-F-K-J-I-C-B, M-F-K-J-P-O-N-B, etc.
    Therefore, a single node removal is not enough to disconnect M and B. The minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2.

5.  **Consider removing two nodes:**
    Let's examine the immediate connections from M. All paths from M to B must pass through one of M's direct neighbors: E, F, or L.
    *   Node **E** is a direct connection between M and B (M-E-B).
    *   Node **F** is a gateway to a large part of the graph that eventually connects to B (e.g., M-F-G-H-B, M-F-K-J-I-C-B, M-F-K-J-P-O-N-B).
    *   Node **L** is connected to M, F, and E. Any path from M through L must then pass through F or E to reach B.

    Consider removing nodes **E** and **F**:
    *   **Removing E:** This immediately breaks the direct path M-E-B. It also breaks any path that uses E as an intermediate node from M (e.g., M-L-E-B).
    *   **After removing E**, M is now only connected to L and F. Since F is also being removed, M will only be connected to L.

4.  **Determine if this cut set is minimal by finding edge-disjoint paths:**

    *   **Path 1:** B - C - H
        Edges: (B, C), (C, H)

    None of the edges in Path 2 are present in Path 1 or Path 3.
    None of the edges in Path 3 are present in Path 1 or Path 2.
    Therefore, these three paths are indeed edge-disjoint.

    According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes required to disconnect H and E (excluding H and E themselves) is equal to the maximum number of node-disjoint paths between them. Since I found 3 node-disjoint paths, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 3.

4.  **Propose a cut set of size 3:** To disconnect these three paths, I need to remove at least one node from each path.
    *   From P1, I can remove B.
    *   From P2, I can remove G or F. Let's choose F.
    *   From P3, I can remove I, J, or M. Let's choose M.

    This gives a candidate cut set: [B, F, M].

5.  **Verify the proposed cut set:** Let's check if removing nodes B, F, and M completely disconnects H from E.
    *   **Removing B:** This immediately breaks Path 1 (H - B - E) and any other path that uses B as an intermediate node (e.g., H-I-C-B-E, H-B-C-I-J-M-E).
    *   **After removing B:**
        *   H is now connected to G and I.
        *   E is now connected to M and F.
    *   **Removing F:** This breaks Path 2 (H - G - F - E). It also breaks all other paths from G to E, as all paths from G to E must pass through F (e.g., H-G-X-F-E, H-G-K-F-E, H-G-K-X-F-E).
    *   **After removing B and F:**
        *   H is now connected to G (but G's paths to E are cut by F) and I.
        *   E is now connected to M.
    *   **Removing M:** This breaks Path 3 (H - I - J - M - E). It also breaks all other paths from I to E, as all paths from I to E must pass through M (e.g., H-I-P-J-M-E, H-I-C-N-O-P-J-M-E).

    Since all identified paths and any other potential paths are broken by removing B, F, and M, this cut set successfully disconnects H and E.

6.  **Conclusion:** Since we found 3 node-disjoint paths, the minimum cut is at least 3. We have also found a cut set of size 3 that successfully disconnects the two black nodes. Therefore, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

Step-by-step reasoning:
The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect the two black nodes, E and J. This is a minimum edge cut problem. According to Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them.

1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The black nodes are E and J.

2.  **Identify all possible paths between E and J:**
    Let's list some of the main paths to understand the connectivity:
    *   P1: E - M - J
    *   P2: E - L - M - J
    *   P3: E - H - I - J
    *   P4: E - H - I - P - J
    *   P5: E - H - B - C - I - J
    *   P6: E - H - B - C - P - J
    *   P7: E - H - B - C - O - P - J
    *   P8: E - G - H - I - J
    *   P9: E - G - H - B - C - I - J
    *   P10: E - G - H - B - C - P - J
    *   P11: E - G - H - B - C - O - P - J
    *   P12: E - G - F - H - I - J (and other paths through F, K, X)

3.  **Attempt to find edge-disjoint paths:**
    To determine the minimum cut, we look for the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths.
    *   **Path 1 (P1): E - M - J**
        Edges: {(E, M), (M, J)}

    *   **Path 2 (P2): E - H - I - J**
        Edges: {(E, H), (H, I), (I, J)}
        P1 and P2 are edge-disjoint (no common edges).

    *   **Path 3 (P3): E - G - H - B - C - P - J**
        Edges: {(E, G), (G, H), (H, B), (B, C), (C, P), (P, J)}
        Let's check if P3 is edge-disjoint from P1 and P2:
        *   P3 vs P1: No common edges. (E,G) vs (E,M), (P,J) vs (M,J).
        *   P3 vs P2: No common edges. (E,G) vs (E,H), (G,H) vs (H,I), (H,B) vs (H,I), (B,C) vs (H,I), (C,P) vs (H,I), (P,J) vs (I,J).
        Therefore, P1, P2, and P3 are three edge-disjoint paths. This implies that the minimum number of edges to remove is at least 3.

4.  **Attempt to find a fourth edge-disjoint path:**
    Edges used so far:
    *   P1: {(E, M), (M, J)}
    *   P2: {(E, H), (H, I), (I, J)}
    *   P3: {(E, G), (G, H), (H, B), (B, C), (C, P), (P, J)}

    Let's look at the remaining outgoing edges from E: (E, L).
    Let's look at the remaining incoming edges to J: (J, D).
    *   Can we form a path using (E, L) and (J, D) that is edge-disjoint from P1, P2, P3?
        E - L - M. The edge (M, J) is used by P1. So, E-L-M-J is not edge-disjoint.
        From M, there are no other paths to J or D without using already used edges or leading to a dead end for J.
        From L, the only connection is to M.

    *   Consider paths from E to D, then D to J.
        E - G - H - B - C - O - P - D - J.
        This path uses (E,G), (G,H), (H,B), (B,C), (P,D), (D,J).
        Edges (E,G), (G,H), (H,B), (B,C) are all used by P3. So this path is not edge-disjoint.

    It appears difficult to find a fourth edge-disjoint path. This suggests that the minimum cut is 3.

5.  **Identify a cut set of 3 edges:**
    Based on the observation that all paths from E must start with one of its direct connections, let's consider cutting these connections.
    The direct connections from E are: (E, G), (E, H), (E, L), (E, M).
    If we remove the edges: **(E, G), (E, H), (E, M)**.
    Let's verify if E is disconnected from J:
    *   Removing (E, G) breaks all paths starting with E-G-...
    *   Removing (E, H) breaks all paths starting with E-H-...
    *   Removing (E, M) breaks all paths starting with E-M-... (including E-M-J).
    *   After these removals, E is only connected to L.
    *   From L, the only connection is to M. However, the edge (E, M) has been removed, so E cannot reach M via L either.
    *   Therefore, E is completely isolated from the rest of the graph, and thus from J.

This cut set of 3 edges successfully disconnects E from J. Since we established that the minimum cut is at least 3 (by finding 3 edge-disjoint paths), and we found a cut set of size 3, this is the minimum.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(E, M), (E, H), (E, G)]

    *   If we remove only 3 edges from this set (e.g., (B, H), (I, H), (P, F)), the edge (E, F) would remain. This would allow paths like N-C-B-E-F-G or N-B-E-F-G to still exist, connecting N to G. Thus, 3 edges are not sufficient.

If any 3 are removed, G remains connected to at least one of H, K, F, or X, which are themselves connected to other parts of the graph (e.g., F is connected to P and E). For example, if (H, G), (K, G), (F, G) are removed, (X, G) remains, and a path like N-O-P-F-X-G could still exist. Therefore, removing 3 edges directly connected to G is also not sufficient.

Both approaches confirm that a minimum of 4 edges are required.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 4, Cut Set: [(B, H), (I, H), (P, F), (E, F)]

Step-by-step reasoning:
1.  **Identify Target Nodes:** The two black nodes are J and E.
2.  **Analyze Connections to Target Node E:** Node E is directly connected to three other nodes: B, L, and F. This means there are three distinct "entry points" into E from the rest of the graph.
    *   Edge (E, B)
    *   Edge (E, L)
    *   Edge (E, F)
3.  **Initial Cut Set (Direct Disconnection):** If we remove all edges directly connected to E, namely (E, B), (E, L), and (E, F), then E becomes an isolated node. Consequently, no path can exist between J and E. This constitutes a cut set of size 3.
4.  **Check for Minimality (Can we do it with fewer edges?):**
    *   **Size 1:** It's impossible to disconnect E with just one edge, as E has three distinct connections. Removing one would leave at least two other paths.
    *   **Size 2:** To determine if a cut of size 2 is possible, we can use Menger's theorem, which states that the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them. Let's try to find edge-disjoint paths from J to E.
        *   **Path 1:** J - M - L - E
            *   Edges: (J, M), (M, L), (L, E)
        *   **Path 2:** J - P - O - N - B - E

        *   **Path 3:** J - I - H - G - K - F - E
            *   Edges: (J, I), (I, H), (H, G), (G, K), (K, F), (F, E)
    *   **Verification of Edge-Disjointness:** Let's check if these three paths share any edges.
        *   Path 1 uses (J,M), (M,L), (L,E).
        *   Path 2 uses (J,P), (P,O), (O,N), (N,B), (B,E).
        *   Path 3 uses (J,I), (I,H), (H,G), (G,K), (K,F), (F,E).
        *   Indeed, these three paths do not share any common edges. They are edge-disjoint.
5.  **Conclusion on Minimality:** Since we have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between J and E, Menger's theorem dictates that at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect them. Our initial cut set of [(E, B), (E, L), (E, F)] achieves this minimum of 3 edges.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(E, B), (E, L), (E, F)]

Image name: SEIS_task3_gt3_PAIR3
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 17
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are E (black) and H (black). My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect E from H.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their immediate connections:**
    *   Node E is connected to: L, M, F, G, B.
    *   Node H is connected to: G, I, B.

2.  **Consider all paths from E to H:**
    Any path from E to H must eventually pass through one of H's direct neighbors: G, I, or B.

3.  **Apply Menger's Theorem (Edge Version):** The minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them. I will try to find edge-disjoint paths from E to H.

    *   **Path 1:** E - G - H
        *   Edges: (E, G), (G, H)

    *   **Path 2:** E - B - H
        *   Edges: (E, B), (B, H)

    *   **Path 3:** E - M - J - P - I - H
        *   Edges: (E, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, I), (I, H)

4.  **Verify edge-disjointness of the identified paths:**
    *   Path 1 uses edges (E, G) and (G, H).
    *   Path 2 uses edges (E, B) and (B, H).
    *   Path 3 uses edges (E, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, I), (I, H).
    All edges in these three paths are distinct. For example, (E, G), (E, B), and (E, M) are distinct starting edges from E. Similarly, (G, H), (B, H), and (I, H) are distinct ending edges to H. The intermediate edges in Path 3 are also distinct from those in Path 1 and Path 2.
    Therefore, there are at least 3 edge-disjoint paths between E and H.

5.  **Determine the minimum number of edges to remove:**
    Since there are 3 edge-disjoint paths, according to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect E and H is 3.

6.  **Identify a minimal cut set:**
    To disconnect H from E, we need to remove edges such that no path can reach H. Since H is only connected to G, B, and I, removing all edges directly connected to H will isolate it.
    *   Edges connected to H: (G, H), (B, H), (I, H).
    *   If we remove these three edges, H becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph, and thus from E.
    *   This cut set has a size of 3, which matches the minimum determined by Menger's Theorem.

7.  **Verify that no smaller cut set exists:**
    *   **Can we disconnect with 1 edge?** No, because we found 3 edge-disjoint paths. Removing one edge can only break one of these paths, leaving at least two others intact.
    *   **Can we disconnect with 2 edges?** No. If we remove any two edges, at least one of the three edge-disjoint paths identified above will remain intact. For example:
        *   If we remove (G, H) and (B, H), Path 3 (E - M - J - P - I - H) still exists.
        *   If we remove (G, H) and (I, H), Path 2 (E - B - H) still exists.
        *   If we remove (B, H) and (I, H), Path 1 (E - G - H) still exists.
        *   If we remove any two edges not directly connected to H, say (E, G) and (E, B), then Path 3 (E - M - J - P - I - H) still exists.

Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(G, H), (B, H), (I, H)]
